href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Catholic_Church_in_Croatia_sidebar" title="Template:Catholic Church in Croatia sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Catholic_Church_in_Croatia_sidebar" title="Template talk:Catholic Church in Croatia sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Catholic_Church_in_Croatia_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Aloysius Viktor <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholic_Church)" title="Cardinal (Catholic Church)">Cardinal</a> Stepinac</b> (<a href="/wiki/Croatian_language" title="Croatian language">Croatian</a>: <i lang="hr">Alojzije Viktor Stepinac</i>, 8 May 1898 – 10 February 1960) was a senior-ranking <a href="/wiki/Yugoslavia" title="Yugoslavia">Yugoslav</a> <a href="/wiki/Croats" title="Croats">Croat</a> <a href="/wiki/Prelate" title="Prelate">prelate</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>. A <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholic_Church)" title="Cardinal (Catholic Church)">cardinal</a>, Stepinac served as <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Zagreb" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Zagreb">Archbishop of Zagreb</a> from 1937 until his death, a period which included the <a href="/wiki/Fascist" class="mw-redirect" title="Fascist">fascist</a> rule of the <a href="/wiki/Usta%C5%A1e" title="Ustaše">Ustaše</a> over the <a href="/wiki/Axis_powers" title="Axis powers">Axis</a> <a href="/wiki/Puppet_state" title="Puppet state">puppet state</a> the <a href="/wiki/Independent_State_of_Croatia" title="Independent State of Croatia">Independent State of Croatia</a> (<a href="/wiki/Croatian_language" title="Croatian language">Croatian</a>: <i lang="hr">Nezavisna Država Hrvatska</i> or NDH) from 1941 to 1945 during <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>. He was tried by the <a href="/wiki/Communist" class="mw-redirect" title="Communist">communist</a> <a href="/wiki/Socialist_Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia" title="Socialist Federal Republic of Yugoslavia">Yugoslav</a> government after the war and convicted of <a href="/wiki/Treason" title="Treason">treason</a> and collaboration with the Ustaše regime.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> The trial was depicted in the West as a typical communist "<a href="/wiki/Show_trial" title="Show trial">show trial</a>",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColeman1991113_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeman1991113-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGruenwald1987516_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGruenwald1987516-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> and was described by <i><a href="/wiki/The_New_York_Times" title="The New York Times">The New York Times</a></i> as biased against the Archbishop (he didn't become a Cardinal until 1953).<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> However, Professor <a href="/wiki/John_Van_Antwerp_Fine_Jr." title="John Van Antwerp Fine Jr.">John Van Antwerp Fine Jr.</a> is of the opinion that the trial was "carried out with proper legal procedure".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> In a verdict that polarized public opinion both in Yugoslavia and beyond,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander1987_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander1987-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> the Yugoslav authorities found him guilty on the charge of <a href="/wiki/High_treason" class="mw-redirect" title="High treason">high treason</a> (for collaboration with the fascist Ustaše regime), as well as complicity in the forced conversions of <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">Orthodox</a> <a href="/wiki/Serbs" title="Serbs">Serbs</a> to <a href="/wiki/Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholicism">Catholicism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhayer2000182_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhayer2000182-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac advised individual priests to admit Orthodox believers to the Catholic Church if their lives were in danger, such that this conversion had no validity, allowing them to return to their faith once the danger passed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKrešić200794_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKrešić200794-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> He was sentenced to 16 years in prison, but served only five at <a href="/wiki/Lepoglava_prison" title="Lepoglava prison">Lepoglava</a> before being transferred to <a href="/wiki/House_arrest" title="House arrest">house arrest</a> with his movements confined to his home <a href="/wiki/Parish" title="Parish">parish</a> of <a href="/wiki/Kra%C5%A1i%C4%87" title="Krašić">Krašić</a>.
</p><p>In 1952 he was designated for elevation to <a href="/wiki/Catholic_cardinal" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic cardinal">cardinal</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a>. He was unable to participate in the <a href="/wiki/1958_papal_conclave" title="1958 papal conclave">1958 conclave</a> due to the house arrest to which he had been sentenced. On 10 February 1960, still under confinement in Krašić, Stepinac died of <a href="/wiki/Polycythemia" title="Polycythemia">polycythemia</a> and other illnesses he contracted while imprisoned.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> On 3 October 1998, <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> declared him a <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a> and <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatified</a> him before 500,000 Croatians in <a href="/wiki/Marija_Bistrica" title="Marija Bistrica">Marija Bistrica</a> near Zagreb.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson199990–92_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson199990–92-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His record during World War II, conviction, and subsequent <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatification</a> remain controversial. On 22 July 2016, the Zagreb County Court annulled his post-war conviction due to "gross violations of current and former fundamental principles of substantive and procedural criminal law".<sup id="cite_ref-index1_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-index1-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pope_Francis" title="Pope Francis">Pope Francis</a> invited <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">Serbian prelates</a> to participate in <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonization</a> investigations, but in 2017 a joint commission was only able to agree that "[i]n the case of Cardinal Stepinac, the interpretations that were predominantly given by Catholic Croats and Orthodox Serbs remain divergent".<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Alojzije Viktor Stepinac was born in <a href="/wiki/Brezari%C4%87" title="Brezarić">Brezarić</a>, a village in the parish of <a href="/wiki/Kra%C5%A1i%C4%87" title="Krašić">Krašić</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Austria-Hungary" title="Austria-Hungary">Austro-Hungarian</a> <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Croatia-Slavonia" title="Kingdom of Croatia-Slavonia">Kingdom of Croatia-Slavonia</a> on 8 May 1898, to a wealthy <a href="/wiki/Viticulture" title="Viticulture">viticulturalist</a>, Josip Stepinac, and his second wife Barbara (<span class="avoidwrap" style="display:inline-block;" title="Name at birth"><a href="/wiki/N%C3%A9e" class="mw-redirect" title="Née">née</a> Penić</span>). He was the fifth of nine children,<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> and he had three more <a href="/wiki/Sibling" title="Sibling">siblings</a> from his father's first marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His mother, a devout <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Roman Catholic</a>, prayed constantly that he would enter the <a href="/wiki/Priesthood_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Priesthood (Catholic Church)">priesthood</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButlerBurns1995263_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButlerBurns1995263-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> The family moved to Krašić in 1906,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman200649_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman200649-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> and Stepinac attended primary school there, then attended high school in <a href="/wiki/Zagreb" title="Zagreb">Zagreb</a> from 1909 to 1915,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Room_and_board" title="Room and board">boarding</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Zagreb" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Zagreb">Archdiocese of Zagreb</a> orphanage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButlerBurns1995263_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButlerBurns1995263-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> This was followed by study at the lycée of the archdiocese, as he was seriously considering taking <a href="/wiki/Holy_orders_in_the_Catholic_Church" title="Holy orders in the Catholic Church">holy orders</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> having sent in his application to the <a href="/wiki/Seminary" title="Seminary">seminary</a> at the age of 16.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButlerBurns1995263_14-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButlerBurns1995263-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was conscripted into the <a href="/wiki/Austro-Hungarian_Army" title="Austro-Hungarian Army">Austro-Hungarian Army</a> for service in <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, and had to accelerate his studies and graduate ahead of schedule. Sent to a reserve officers school in <a href="/wiki/Rijeka" title="Rijeka">Rijeka</a>, after six months training he was sent to serve on the <a href="/wiki/Italian_Campaign_(World_War_I)" class="mw-redirect" title="Italian Campaign (World War I)">Italian Front</a> in 1917 where he commanded <a href="/wiki/Bosnia_(region)" title="Bosnia (region)">Bosnian</a> soldiers. In July 1918, he was captured by Italian forces who held him as a <a href="/wiki/Prisoner_of_war" title="Prisoner of war">prisoner of war</a>. His family was initially told that he had been killed, and a memorial service held in Krašić. A week after the service, his parents received a telegram from their son telling them he had been captured. He was held in various Italian <a href="/wiki/Prisoner-of-war_camp" title="Prisoner-of-war camp">prisoner-of-war camps</a> until 6 December 1918.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II_17-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the formation of the <a href="/wiki/State_of_Slovenes,_Croats_and_Serbs" title="State of Slovenes, Croats and Serbs">State of Slovenes, Croats and Serbs</a> on 1 December 1918, he was no longer treated as an enemy soldier, and he volunteered for the Yugoslav Legion that had been engaged on the <a href="/wiki/Salonika_front" class="mw-redirect" title="Salonika front">Salonika front</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II_17-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> As the war had already ended, he was demobilized with the rank of <a href="/wiki/Second_lieutenant" title="Second lieutenant">second lieutenant</a> and returned home in the spring of 1919.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the war he enrolled at the Faculty of Agriculture at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Zagreb" title="University of Zagreb">University of Zagreb</a>, but left it after only one semester and returned home to help his father in his vineyards.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> His father wanted him to marry, and in 1923 he was briefly engaged to a teacher, Marija Horvat, but the engagement was broken off.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II_17-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> In 1922, Stepinac was part of the politically conservative Catholic <i>Hrvatski orlovi</i> (Croatian Eagles) youth sport organisation, and traveled to the <a href="/wiki/Mass_games" title="Mass games">mass games</a> in <a href="/wiki/Brno" title="Brno">Brno</a>, <a href="/wiki/Czechoslovakia" title="Czechoslovakia">Czechoslovakia</a>. He was at the front of the group's ceremonial procession, carrying the <a href="/wiki/Croatian_flag" class="mw-redirect" title="Croatian flag">Croatian flag</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELampe2004105_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELampe2004105-23">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 28 October 1924, at the age of 26, Stepinac entered the <a href="/wiki/Collegium_Germanicum_et_Hungaricum" title="Collegium Germanicum et Hungaricum">Collegium Germanicum et Hungaricum</a> in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> to study for the priesthood.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila_18-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> During his studies there he befriended the future Austrian cardinal <a href="/wiki/Franz_K%C3%B6nig" title="Franz König">Franz König</a> when the two played together on a volleyball team.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKonig200536_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKonig200536-24">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Granted an American scholarship, he went on to study for doctorates in both theology and philosophy at the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_Gregorian_University" title="Pontifical Gregorian University">Pontifical Gregorian University</a>. Along with Croatian, he was fluent in Italian, German and French.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II_17-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was ordained on 26 October 1930 by Archbishop <a href="/wiki/Giuseppe_Palica" title="Giuseppe Palica">Giuseppe Palica</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vicegerent" title="Vicegerent">Vicegerent</a> of Rome, in a ceremony which also included the ordination of his eventual successor as Archbishop of Zagreb, <a href="/wiki/Franjo_%C5%A0eper" title="Franjo Šeper">Franjo Šeper</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila_18-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> On 1 November, he said his first <a href="/wiki/Mass_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mass (Catholic Church)">mass</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_di_Santa_Maria_Maggiore" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica di Santa Maria Maggiore">Basilica di Santa Maria Maggiore</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila_18-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac wanted to serve the common people, and wanted to be a parish priest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman200649_15-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman200649-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He celebrated his first mass in his home parish of Krašić on 1 July 1931, but instead of being appointed to a parish he was appointed as <a href="/wiki/Liturgy" title="Liturgy">liturgical</a> <a href="/wiki/Master_of_ceremonies" title="Master of ceremonies">master of ceremonies</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Zagreb" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Zagreb">Archbishop of Zagreb</a> <a href="/wiki/Antun_Bauer_(archbishop)" title="Antun Bauer (archbishop)">Antun Bauer</a> on 1 October. He also established the archdiocesan branch of the Catholic charity <a href="/wiki/Caritas_Internationalis" title="Caritas Internationalis">Caritas</a> in December of that year,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila_18-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> and initiated and edited the Caritas magazine.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman200650_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman200650-25">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> He also temporarily administered the parishes of <a href="/wiki/Samobor" title="Samobor">Samobor</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sveti_Ivan_Zelina" title="Sveti Ivan Zelina">Sveti Ivan Zelina</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II_17-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> By this time, Stepinac had become a strong Croatian nationalist, but was not active in <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Action" title="Catholic Action">Catholic Action</a> or the politically conservative <a href="/wiki/Croatian_Catholic_movement" title="Croatian Catholic movement">Croatian Catholic movement</a>. He was considered "conscientious and devoted to his work".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001552_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001552-26">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Coadjutor_archbishop">Coadjutor archbishop</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Coadjutor archbishop">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Marija_Bistrica.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/Marija_Bistrica.jpg/220px-Marija_Bistrica.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="330" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/Marija_Bistrica.jpg/330px-Marija_Bistrica.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/Marija_Bistrica.jpg/440px-Marija_Bistrica.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1295" data-file-height="1945" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Marija_Bistrica.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Black Madonna of Marija Bistrica, to which Stepinac led a pilgrimage soon after his consecration</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Appointment">Appointment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Appointment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Stepinac was appointed <a href="/wiki/Coadjutor_bishop" title="Coadjutor bishop">coadjutor bishop</a> to Bauer on 28 May 1934 at the age of 36 years, having been a priest for only three-and-a-half years, being selected after all other candidates had been rejected. Both <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a> and King <a href="/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Yugoslavia" title="Alexander I of Yugoslavia">Alexander I of Yugoslavia</a> agreed with his appointment, and although the king wanted to withdraw his assent after he received further information about Stepinac, he was dissuaded by Bauer. According to some sources, Stepinac was the fifth or even eighth candidate to be considered for the role, which brought with it the right to succeed Bauer. Stepinac's decision to join the Yugoslav Legion in 1918 made him a more acceptable candidate to King Alexander.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001552_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001552-26">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Stepinac's biographer, Friar Šimun Ćorić, Bauer asked Stepinac if he would give his formal consent to being named as Bauer's successor, but after considering the issue for several days, Stepinac refused, saying that he considered himself unfit to be appointed as a bishop. In this version of events, Bauer persisted, and once it was clear that King Alexander had agreed to his appointment, Stepinac consented.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II_17-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Upon his naming, he took <i>In te, Domine, speravi</i> (I place my trust in You, my Lord) as his motto.
</p><p>At the time of his consecration on 24 June 1934,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila_18-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac was the youngest bishop in the Catholic Church,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButlerBurns1995263_14-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButlerBurns1995263-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> and was completely unknown to the Croat people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II_17-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Two weeks after his consecration, he led a 15,000-strong pilgrimage to the old <a href="/wiki/Marian_shrine" class="mw-redirect" title="Marian shrine">Marian shrine</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Black_Madonna" title="Black Madonna">Black Madonna</a> at <a href="/wiki/Marija_Bistrica" title="Marija Bistrica">Marija Bistrica</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButlerBurns1995263_14-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButlerBurns1995263-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac followed this with annual pilgrimages to the site.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199582_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199582-27">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Bauer delegated many tasks and responsibilities to Stepinac, and he travelled widely within the country.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman200650_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman200650-25">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_situation">Political situation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Political situation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Stepinac's appointment came at a time of acute political turmoil in Yugoslavia. In June 1928, the popular leader of the <a href="/wiki/Croatian_Peasant_Party" title="Croatian Peasant Party">Croatian Peasant Party</a> (<a href="/wiki/Gaj%27s_Latin_alphabet" title="Gaj&#39;s Latin alphabet">Serbo-Croatian Latin</a>: <i lang="sh-Latn">Hrvatska seljačka stranka</i>, HSS) <a href="/wiki/Stjepan_Radi%C4%87" title="Stjepan Radić">Stjepan Radić</a> and several other Croatian <a href="/wiki/Deputy_(legislator)" class="mw-redirect" title="Deputy (legislator)">deputies</a> had been shot by a <a href="/wiki/Serbs" title="Serbs">Serb</a> deputy in the Yugoslav Parliament. Two had died immediately and Radić had succumbed to his wounds two months later, the incident causing widespread outrage among Croats.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich200124–26_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich200124–26-28">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> In January of the following year, King Alexander had <a href="/wiki/Legislative_session" title="Legislative session">prorogued</a> Parliament and had effectively become a royal dictator.
</p><p>In April 1933, the new leader of the HSS <a href="/wiki/Vladko_Ma%C4%8Dek" title="Vladko Maček">Vladko Maček</a> had been sent to prison for three years on charges of <a href="/wiki/Separatism" title="Separatism">separatism</a> after he and other opposition figures had issued the <a href="/wiki/Zagreb_Points" title="Zagreb Points">Zagreb Points</a> condemning the royal regime and its policies. While Maček was in prison, his deputy <a href="/wiki/Josip_Predavec" title="Josip Predavec">Josip Predavec</a> was apparently murdered by the police.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich200129_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich200129-29">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> When Stepinac wanted to visit Maček in prison to thank him for his well-wishes on Stepinac's appointment as coadjutor bishop, his request was denied.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJanjatović2002285_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJanjatović2002285-30">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In response to the many messages of support, Stepinac "was sincerely thankful for all the congratulations, but said that he was not enthusiastic about the appointment because it was too heavy a cross for him".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II_17-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆorić1998II-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 30 July 1934, Stepinac received the French deputy <a href="/wiki/Robert_Schuman" title="Robert Schuman">Robert Schuman</a>, whom he told: "There is no justice in Yugoslavia.&#160;... The Catholic Church endures much".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGabelica200775_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabelica200775-31">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Throughout 1934, Stepinac spoke with veteran Croatian politician and <a href="/wiki/De_facto" title="De facto">de facto</a> head of the HSS <a href="/wiki/Ante_Trumbi%C4%87" title="Ante Trumbić">Ante Trumbić</a> on several occasions. On his views regarding the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia" title="Kingdom of Yugoslavia">Kingdom of Yugoslavia</a>, Trumbić recorded that Stepinac had
</p>
<blockquote><p>loyalty to the state as it is, but with the condition that the state acts towards the Catholic Church as it does to all just denominations and that it guarantees them freedom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGabelica200786_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabelica200786-32">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>After his consecration, Stepinac visited Belgrade to pledge his allegiance to King Alexander. The journalist <a href="/wiki/Richard_West_(journalist)" title="Richard West (journalist)">Richard West</a> quotes Stepinac:
</p>
<blockquote><p>I told the King that I was not a politician and that I would forbid my clergy to take part in party politics, but on the other hand I would look for full respect for the rights of Croats. I warned the King that the Croats must not be improperly provoked and even forbidden to use the very name of Croat, something which I had myself experienced.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199560–61_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199560–61-33">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>On 9 October 1934, King Alexander was assassinated in <a href="/wiki/Marseilles" class="mw-redirect" title="Marseilles">Marseilles</a>  by a <a href="/wiki/Bulgarians" title="Bulgarians">Bulgarian</a> gunman backed by the Croatian nationalist organisation, the <a href="/wiki/Usta%C5%A1e" title="Ustaše">Ustaše</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich200132–33_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich200132–33-34">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac, along with Bishops <a href="/w/index.php?title=Antun_Ak%C5%A1amovi%C4%87&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Antun Akšamović (page does not exist)">Antun Akšamović</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dionizije_Njaradi" class="mw-redirect" title="Dionizije Njaradi">Dionizije Njaradi</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gregorij_Ro%C5%BEman" title="Gregorij Rožman">Gregorij Rožman</a>, were given special permission by the <a href="/wiki/Nuncio" title="Nuncio">Papal Nuncio</a> in Belgrade to attend the <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">Serbian Orthodox</a> funeral.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEŽutić2000518_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEŽutić2000518-35">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Less than a month after the assassination, Stepinac was among those who signed what became known as the "Zagreb Memorandum",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPerić2003174–175_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerić2003174–175-36">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> which listed a number of demands, including the <a href="/wiki/Exoneration" title="Exoneration">exoneration</a> of Maček, a general amnesty, freedom of movement and association, restrictions on the activities of government-authorised paramilitaries, and free elections. The key demand of the Memorandum was that the <a href="/wiki/Regency" class="mw-redirect" title="Regency">regency</a> that had succeeded the king should address the "Croatian question",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENielsen2014240–241_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENielsen2014240–241-37">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> the desire of many Croats for self-determination.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich200115_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich200115-38">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_activities">Other activities</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Other activities">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1936, he climbed Mount <a href="/wiki/Triglav" title="Triglav">Triglav</a>, the tallest peak in Yugoslavia. In 2006, the 70th anniversary of his climb was commemorated with a memorial chapel being built near the summit.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZagorac2006_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZagorac2006-39">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> In July 1937,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila_18-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlas_Koncila-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> he led a pilgrimage to the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Land" title="Holy Land">Holy Land</a> (then the <a href="/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine" title="Mandatory Palestine">British Mandate of Palestine</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEČelar2006_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEČelar2006-40">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> During the pilgrimage, he blessed an altar dedicated to the martyr <a href="/wiki/Nikola_Taveli%C4%87" class="mw-redirect" title="Nikola Tavelić">Nikola Tavelić</a>, who had already been <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatified</a> at that time, and was later <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonised</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESKAC2010_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESKAC2010-41">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> After his return from Palestine, Stepinac began a campaign for the canonisation of Tavelić, and proposed that a monument to him be built in the <a href="/wiki/Velebit" title="Velebit">Velebit</a> mountains overlooking the <a href="/wiki/Adriatic_Sea" title="Adriatic Sea">Adriatic Sea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199583_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199583-42">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Archbishop_of_Zagreb">Archbishop of Zagreb</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Archbishop of Zagreb">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Locator_map_Croatia_Banovina_in_Yugoslavia_1939-1941.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Locator_map_Croatia_Banovina_in_Yugoslavia_1939-1941.svg/220px-Locator_map_Croatia_Banovina_in_Yugoslavia_1939-1941.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="191" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Locator_map_Croatia_Banovina_in_Yugoslavia_1939-1941.svg/330px-Locator_map_Croatia_Banovina_in_Yugoslavia_1939-1941.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Locator_map_Croatia_Banovina_in_Yugoslavia_1939-1941.svg/440px-Locator_map_Croatia_Banovina_in_Yugoslavia_1939-1941.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1220" data-file-height="1057" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Locator_map_Croatia_Banovina_in_Yugoslavia_1939-1941.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The creation of the Banovina of Croatia was Prince Paul's attempt to address the "Croatian question"</div></div></div>
<p>On 7 December 1937, Bauer died, and though still below the age of forty, Stepinac succeeded him as Archbishop of Zagreb. Presaging the Ustaše reign of terror during <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>, Stepinac addressed a group of university students during <a href="/wiki/Lent" title="Lent">Lent</a> in 1938, saying,
</p>
<blockquote><p>Love for one's own nation must not turn a man into a wild animal, which destroys everything and calls for reprisal, but it must enrich him, so that his own nation respects and loves other nations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomić199867_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomić199867-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In 1938, the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia_parliamentary_election,_1938" class="mw-redirect" title="Kingdom of Yugoslavia parliamentary election, 1938">Kingdom of Yugoslavia held its last election</a> before the outbreak of war. Stepinac voted for Maček's opposition list, while <a href="/wiki/Radio_Belgrade" title="Radio Belgrade">Radio Belgrade</a> spread the false information that he had voted for <a href="/wiki/Milan_Stojadinovi%C4%87" title="Milan Stojadinović">Milan Stojadinović</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Yugoslav_Radical_Union" title="Yugoslav Radical Union">Yugoslav Radical Union</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHorvat1996154_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHorvat1996154-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In the latter half of 1938, Stepinac had an operation for acute appendicitis.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander198754_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander198754-45">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1940, Stepinac received the regent <a href="/wiki/Prince_Paul_of_Yugoslavia" title="Prince Paul of Yugoslavia">Prince Paul</a> at <a href="/wiki/St._Mark%27s_Church,_Zagreb" title="St. Mark&#39;s Church, Zagreb">St. Mark's Church</a> as he arrived in Zagreb to garner support for the 1939 <a href="/wiki/Cvetkovi%C4%87%E2%80%93Ma%C4%8Dek_Agreement" title="Cvetković–Maček Agreement">Cvetković–Maček Agreement</a>, which had created the autonomous <a href="/wiki/Banovina_of_Croatia" title="Banovina of Croatia">Banovina of Croatia</a> within Yugoslavia. The Agreement was intended to address the "Croatian question", but did not satisfy those demanding full independence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETanner1997135_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanner1997135-46">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Pope <a href="/wiki/Pius_XII" class="mw-redirect" title="Pius XII">Pius XII</a> declared the period from 29 June 1940 to 29 June 1941 as a <a href="/wiki/Jubilee_(Christian)" class="mw-redirect" title="Jubilee (Christian)">jubilee</a> year to celebrate 1300 years of Christianity among the Croats.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEŽutić2001419_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEŽutić2001419-47">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> In 1940, the <a href="/wiki/Franciscan_Order" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan Order">Franciscan Order</a> celebrated 700 years in Croatia and the order's <a href="/wiki/Minister_General_(Franciscan)" title="Minister General (Franciscan)">Minister General</a> <a href="/w/index.php?title=Leonardo_Bello&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Leonardo Bello (page does not exist)">Leonardo Bello</a> came to Zagreb for the event. During his visit, Stepinac joined the <a href="/wiki/Third_Order_of_Saint_Francis" title="Third Order of Saint Francis">Third Order of Saint Francis</a>, on 29 September 1940.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander198726–27_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander198726–27-48">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> After the death of Bauer, Stepinac attempted to remain aloof from politics, and tried to unify Croatian Catholic organisations and subordinate them directly to his authority. He was unable to achieve this, probably because he was young and relatively inexperienced, and did not command the level of respect and authority usually accorded an Archbishop of Zagreb.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a40_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a40-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The historian Mark Biondich observes that the Catholic Church had historically been on the fringes of Croatian mass politics and public life, and that the influence of the Church had been further eroded during the <a href="/wiki/Interwar_period" title="Interwar period">interwar period</a> due to the royal dictatorship and the popularity of the anti-clerical HSS.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a41_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a41-50">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_and_religious_views_prior_to_World_War_II">Political and religious views prior to World War II</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Political and religious views prior to World War II">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:215px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Kathedrale_von_Zagreb.jpg" class="image"><img alt="a Gothic cathedral" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/98/Kathedrale_von_Zagreb.jpg" decoding="async" width="213" height="327" class="thumbimage" data-file-width="213" data-file-height="327" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Kathedrale_von_Zagreb.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Zagreb Cathedral</div></div></div>
<p>During his period as coadjutor archbishop and as Archbishop of Zagreb up to the <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">German</a>-led <a href="/wiki/Axis_powers" title="Axis powers">Axis</a> <a href="/wiki/Invasion_of_Yugoslavia" title="Invasion of Yugoslavia">invasion of Yugoslavia</a> in April 1941, Stepinac made his views clear on a number of political and religious issues. Foremost among these statements were those regarding <a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestantism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern orthodoxy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Communism" title="Communism">communism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Freemasonry" title="Freemasonry">Freemasonry</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001552_26-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001552-26">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stepinac criticized Protestantism, stating in a speech in 1938 that "the Catholic Church was the greatest civilising force in human history",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001552_26-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001552-26">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> and railed against those that wanted to deprive the Catholic Church of any influence in public life. He referred to the <a href="/wiki/Reformation" title="Reformation">Reformation</a> as the "Deformation", and denounced <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Luther</a> as a false prophet who "demolished the principles of legal authority given by the Lord".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> He went on to blame Protestantism for the "hell in which human society suffers today",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553_51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> and said that it had opened the road to "anarchy in all forms of human life."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553_51-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac was also highly critical of Eastern Orthodoxy, seeing it as a serious danger to both the Catholic Church and Croats in general. The day after the <a href="/wiki/Yugoslav_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="Yugoslav coup d&#39;état">Yugoslav coup d'état</a> of 27 March 1941, carried out by British-supported Serb officers against a pact with the <a href="/wiki/Axis_powers" title="Axis powers">Axis powers</a>, he wrote in his diary:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553_51-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>All in all, Croats and Serbs are two worlds, the north and south poles, which will never become close except by a miracle of God. The <a href="/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism" title="East–West Schism">schism</a> is the greatest curse of Europe, almost greater than Protestantism. In it there is no morality, no principle, no truth, no justice, no honesty.</p></blockquote>
<p>On the same day he issued an encyclical to his clergy, calling on them to pray for the young king, and that Croatia and Yugoslavia would be "spared the horrors of war". This was consistent with long-standing practice of the Catholic Church to show loyalty to the state and its leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a41_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a41-50">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stepinac was well aware of the fact that an estimated 200,000 mostly Croatian Catholics had converted to the Serbian Orthodox Church in the interwar period. Actual census data contradict these claims, showing slightly greater percentage increase in the number of Catholic Croats compared to Orthodox Serbs in both Croatia and Bosnia.<sup id="cite_ref-:5_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:6_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> He later claimed that Catholics were forced to convert to Orthodoxy during the period between the wars, but according to the historian <a href="/wiki/Jozo_Tomasevich" title="Jozo Tomasevich">Jozo Tomasevich</a>, the principal reason for their conversions was the pro-Serb public policy in the Serb-dominated Yugoslav state meant that it was advantageous both politically and for career prospects to be a member of the dominant religion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001524_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001524-54">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Tomasevich also notes that despite the fact that it was the national church of the dominant nation in the country, the Serb Orthodox Church felt threatened by Rome, particularly in Bosnia where the Catholic Church was extremely dynamic - in just one diocese, encompassing half of Bosnia's Catholics, it created 17 new parishes in the interwar period.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001527_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001527-55">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Tomasevich cites <a href="/wiki/Vladko_Ma%C4%8Dek" title="Vladko Maček">Vladko Maček</a>, the leading prewar Croatian opposition leader, who when attacked by an Ustaše priest, for failing to state in his autobiography that the Catholic Church was persecuted in prewar Yugoslavia, Maček responded: "I could not write about the persecution of the Catholic Church because to the best of my knowledge such persecution did not exist."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001524–525_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001524–525-56">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Yet, Stepinac viewed the Yugoslav state as essentially anti-Catholic, particularly after the failure of the Yugoslav Parliament to ratify the already signed <a href="/wiki/Concordat" title="Concordat">Concordat</a> with the Vatican, which would have put the Catholic Church on a more equal footing with the Orthodox Church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a40_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a40-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> He was also sensitive to the fact that the Concordat had been vetoed in the Yugoslav parliament partly due to pressure exerted by the Serbian church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001525_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001525-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The political scientist <a href="/wiki/Sabrina_P._Ramet" title="Sabrina P. Ramet">Sabrina P. Ramet</a> has detailed a range of anti-Catholic aspects to the interwar Yugoslav state, including that King Alexander tried to regulate the religious life of the state, entrenching discrimination against adherents of religious groups other than the Serbian Orthodox Church, and trying to erode their influence by allowing Orthodox proselytising in Catholic areas. Yet Stella Alexander states that both the Catholic and Orthodox churches proselytized in interwar years, with the Catholic Church doing so more openly and aggressively than the Orthodox, especially in Bosnia Hercegovina which had a mixed Catholic, Muslim and Orthodox population.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197881_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197881-58">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> The press was used to accuse the Catholic Church of being pro-fascist, despite the Yugoslav government itself having fascist traits at the time. The government promoted the <a href="/wiki/Old_Catholic_Church" title="Old Catholic Church">Old Catholic Church</a>, a rival organisation which has been established when some Catholics refused to accept the doctrine of <a href="/wiki/Papal_infallibility" title="Papal infallibility">papal infallibility</a> after the <a href="/wiki/First_Vatican_Council" title="First Vatican Council">First Vatican Council</a>. Despite the fact that in 1921, Catholics made up 39.3 per cent of the population, and Orthodox comprised 46.7 per cent, the Ministry of Faiths initially allocated fourteen times more money to the Orthodox Church than the Catholic Church. While this was adjusted, the funding proportions remained very inequitable. All of these strategies worked to undermine the role of the Catholic Church in Yugoslavia. The promotion of Serbdom was at the centre of government education policy, with school books promoting the importance of Serbian Orthodox monasteries in Dalmatia while ignoring Catholic ones. The official newspaper of the Serbian Orthodox Church stated that it wanted to achieve the "victory of Serbian Orthodoxy" throughout Yugoslavia. Under the constant pressure from the state and the Serbian Orthodox Church, between 1923 and 1931 the proportion of Catholics in Yugoslavia declined to 37.4 per cent and that of Serbian Orthodox believers increased to 48.7 per cent. The Croatian theologian Roko Rogošić claimed that 100,000 Roman Catholics had converted to Eastern Orthodoxy under pressure from the Yugoslav government and the Serbian Orthodox Church in 1935 alone.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamet200694–96_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamet200694–96-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> However, census data contradict these claims, showing a slightly greater percentage increase in the number of Catholic Croats compared to Orthodox Serbs in both Croatia<sup id="cite_ref-:5_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> and Bosnia<sup id="cite_ref-:6_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> in the interwar years.
</p><p>In 1930's Croatia the Catholic movement shifted rightward toward authoritarian, radical Catholicism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFroehlich2007387_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFroehlich2007387-60">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Under Stepinac the hierarchical <i>Crusaders</i> Catholic youth organization grew to 40,000 members by 1938. Via uniformed parades and public rallies, and their slogan "God, Church, Homeland", they blended radical Catholicism and Croat nationalism, opposing liberalism, communism and Greater Serbianism<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> They adopted a <a href="/wiki/Greater_Croatia" title="Greater Croatia">Greater Croatia</a> ideology, which envisaged Croatia expanding its borders to claim <a href="/wiki/Bosnia_and_Herzegovina" title="Bosnia and Herzegovina">Bosnia-Herzegovina</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ba%C4%8Dka" title="Bačka">Bačka</a>. In 1941 the Crusaders became enthusiastic supporters of the Ustaše regime.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFroehlich2007393_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFroehlich2007393-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac was the leader of the Croatian <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Action" title="Catholic Action">Catholic Action</a> in the Zagreb Archdiocese, whose newspaper, <i>Hrvatska straža (Croatian Guard)</i> proclaimed they are "always radical Croats and always radical Catholics", that "communism is the greatest evil" and "the fruit of the Jew Karl Marx".<sup id="cite_ref-:3_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> In the Spanish Civil War they sided with Franco, Italy and Germany, because "they do not tolerate Jews or communists or insidious, dangerous Freemasonry." <i>Katolički list</i>, the official newspaper of Stepinac's archdiocese, proclaimed: “Jews are the main enemies of humanity. They are the true curse of the human race."<sup id="cite_ref-:3_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> On the domestic front, Catholic newspapers especially attacked the politics of the <a href="/wiki/Croatian_Peasants_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Croatian Peasants Party">Croatian Peasants Party</a> and <a href="/wiki/Vladko_Ma%C4%8Dek" title="Vladko Maček">Vladko Maček</a> in particular.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_63-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> In the late 1930s a significant segment of the Croatian Catholic movement attacked the Croatian Peasant Party's commitment to democracy, pacifism and negotiation, and instead moved toward independence and authoritarianism, with prominent Catholic intellectuals joining the Ustaše before WWII.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFroehlich2007388–391_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFroehlich2007388–391-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> After 1941, these Catholic activists - men like Ivo Guberina, Milivoj Magdić, Ivan Oršanić, Ivo Bogdan and others - became leading Ustaše propagandists and apologists, some served as officials in the Ustaše regime.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFroehlich2007388–391_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFroehlich2007388–391-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Thus a leader of the Crusaders, Ivan Oršanić, first led the State Secretariat for Propaganda in the NDH, then became the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Usta%C5%A1e_Youth" title="Ustaše Youth">Ustaše Youth</a>, reporting directly to <a href="/wiki/Ante_Paveli%C4%87" title="Ante Pavelić">Pavelić</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFroehlich2007393_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFroehlich2007393-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYeomans2013251_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYeomans2013251-65">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1937 Archbishop Stepinac founded the Committee for Refugee Assistance in Zagreb, which extensively helped the Jews fleeing from Nazi Germany. Terezija Skringar, the secretary of that Committee, would be apprehended by Gestapo immediately after the arrival of Wehrmacht forces in Zagreb in April 1940, and spent 5 months in German detention. Stepinac preached against racism in several sermons, starting from 1938.  "To consider oneself as some higher beings - superhuman and despise another, when it is known that all men are in themselves dust and ashes, and by the mercy of God, all the children of one Heavenly Father", he preached in 1938. Stepinac considered Nazis as „pagans“, and always held a reserved attitude, in any contact with German representatives. Gestapo in Zagreb reported that Stepinac held a clear antipathy against Nazism, and made numerous acts to help prosecuted Jews 1941–1945.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1940, Stepinac had told Prince Paul:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECornwell2008265_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECornwell2008265-67">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>The most ideal thing would be for the Serbs to return to the faith of their fathers, that is, to bow the head before Christ's representative, the Holy Father. Then we could at last breathe in this part of Europe, for Byzantinism has played a frightful role in the history of this part of the world.</p></blockquote>
<p>Of all the threats he perceived to the Croatian people and the Catholic Church, Stepinac railed most against the dangers of communism. In August 1940, in response to the recent establishment of diplomatic relations between Yugoslavia and the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a>, Stepinac sermonised that there could be no co-operation between the Church and communists, stated that the Church was not afraid of communists, and that communists would make Croatia "a nation of killers and robbers, debauchees, and thieves".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553_51-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stepinac was particularly obsessed with Freemasonry,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a40_49-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a40-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> which was closely associated with support for the unification of Yugoslavia and opposed what it considered the Catholic Church's "authoritarianism and anti-liberal ideology."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001554_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001554-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> In 1934 Stepinac wrote in his diary: "In Yugoslavia, today, Freemasonry rules. Unfortunately, in the heart of the Croatian nation also, in Zagreb, this hellish society has entrenched itself, a lair of immorality, corruption, and all kinds of dishonesty, the sworn enemy of the Catholic Church and therefore also of the Croatian nation. Without the knowledge and approval of the Freemasons, nobody can be appointed to any influential position. It is no joke to join battle with it, but it must be done in the interests of the church, the Croatian people, and even the state of Yugoslavia if it wants to continue to exist, because the violence that rules today is supported by Freemasonry."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553–554_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001553–554-69">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tomasevich notes that such vehement sentiments against Freemasonry were was not unusual among conservative senior churchmen prior to the <a href="/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council" title="Second Vatican Council">Second Vatican Council</a>. Tomasevich further observes that despite papal <a href="/wiki/Encyclical" title="Encyclical">encyclicals</a> against both Italian fascist abuses against Catholic Youth organizations in 1931<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> and German Nazism in 1937, Stepinac refrained from condemning or even mentioning Fascism or Nazism, pointing out that in 1938 the Catholic Church was supporting the Italian and German allies of Franco <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War" title="Spanish Civil War">Spanish Civil War</a>, and public criticism of their political ideologies would not have been helpful. Finally, Tomasevich stresses that the Vatican saw Germany as the most important opponent of communism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001554_68-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001554-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, Stepinac was a member of the Yugoslav Catholic Bishops' Conference that issued warnings against both Nazism and Communism after the 1937 papal encyclical against Nazis ideology.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a40_49-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a40-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac feared both Nazism and communism, even as he disdained western parliamentary democracy. This can be seen from Stepinac's diary entry of 5 November 1940, when he wrote,
</p>
<blockquote><p>If Germany wins [the war], there will be appalling terror and the destruction of little nations. If England wins, the masons, [and] Jews will remain in power&#160;... If the USSR wins, then the devil will have authority over both the world and hell.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a40_49-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a40-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>West describes Stepinac as a "puritanical zealot",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199560_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199560-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> who gathered together those opposing communism, <a href="/wiki/Liberalism" title="Liberalism">liberalism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Secular_education" title="Secular education">secular education</a>, <a href="/wiki/Divorce" title="Divorce">divorce</a> reform, <a href="/wiki/Profanity" title="Profanity">profanity</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sexual_intercourse" title="Sexual intercourse">sexual intercourse</a> outside of marriage, and <a href="/wiki/Birth_control" title="Birth control">birth control</a>, under the umbrella of the Croatian Catholic movement. Stepinac even railed against "mixed sunbathing and swimming".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199560_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199560-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> West also observes that by 1934, Stepinac had developed into an
</p>
<blockquote><p>ardent, almost obsessive, Croatian nationalist whose bigotry was softened only by his piety and a measure of human kindness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199582_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199582-27">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>According to the journalist Marcus Tanner, by the time he became coadjutor bishop, Stepinac had become a determined opponent of the Serb-centric approach of the Yugoslav government, and by the time he became archbishop he was a strong supporter of the HSS, making it clear that he had voted for Maček in the 1938 elections.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETanner1997145_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanner1997145-72">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stella Alexander wrote of Stepinac's political outlook:
</p>
<blockquote><p>He was in many ways a typical son of the Church in Croatia of that time, fervidly pious, narrow and dogmatic, believing, in his own words, that "Jews, freemasons and communists" were "the worst enemies of the Church", and that the Orthodox Serbs, the schismatics must whenever possible be brought back to the true Church. This was coupled not only with great courage, but with social concern and charity, especially when he was confronted with individual cases; he was a good pastor and felt close to his people. The impression he makes is also, unexpectedly, one of simplicity and personal modesty. He was conscious of the dignity and weight of his office but never of himself. His courage, which was always great, increased as the pressures on him grew heavier and in the end could be described as heroic; this and his devotion to duty made flight or even withdrawal from his diocese unthinkable. But his political short-sightedness limited his grasp of the apocalyptic events of 1941 to the immediate future of Croatia and the Catholic Church in Croatia, and this left him open to the charge of complicity in the terrible crimes of the ustaše. The same blinkered outlook kept him from establishing some kind of modus Vivendi with the new government immediately after the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander1978_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander1978-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="World_War_II">World War II</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: World War II">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<li><a href="/wiki/108_Martyrs_of_World_War_II" title="108 Martyrs of World War II">108 Blessed Polish Martyrs</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII_and_Poland" title="Pope Pius XII and Poland">Policies</a></li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/In_Poloniae_annalibus" title="In Poloniae annalibus">In Poloniae annalibus</a></i></li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Gloriosam_Reginam" title="Gloriosam Reginam">Gloriosam Reginam</a></i></li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Invicti_athletae" title="Invicti athletae">Invicti athletae</a></i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Jerzy_Popie%C5%82uszko" title="Jerzy Popiełuszko">Jerzy Popiełuszko</a></li></ul></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content hlist">

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content hlist">
<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title"><a href="/wiki/Norway" title="Norway">Norway</a></div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content">
