</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content hlist">
<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title"><a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a></div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/2021_Canadian_church_burnings" title="2021 Canadian church burnings">2021 Canadian church burnings</a></li></ul></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below">
<a href="/wiki/File:046CupolaSPietro.jpg" class="image"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/16px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg" decoding="async" width="16" height="12" class="noviewer thumbborder" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/24px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/32px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="600" /></a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Portal:Catholicism">Catholicism&#32;portal</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"/><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Persecutions_of_the_Catholic_Church_sidebar" title="Template:Persecutions of the Catholic Church sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Persecutions_of_the_Catholic_Church_sidebar" title="Template talk:Persecutions of the Catholic Church sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Persecutions_of_the_Catholic_Church_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>After the outbreak of war in September 1939, the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia" title="Kingdom of Yugoslavia">Kingdom of Yugoslavia</a> declared its neutrality, and the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">United Kingdom</a> worked hard to help Yugoslavia maintain its stance.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStarič200533_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStarič200533-74">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> In the face of steadily mounting pressure from Germany and Italy, by March 1941 Yugoslavia had been completely surrounded by members of the Axis.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoberts19876–7_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoberts19876–7-75">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> In this situation, some senior government figures were advocating for Yugoslavia to also join the <a href="/wiki/Tripartite_Pact" title="Tripartite Pact">Tripartite Pact</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich197530_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich197530-76">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After a number of delays, Prince Paul and Prime Minister Cvetković signed the Pact on 25 March, but the following day there were demonstrations in Belgrade, with protesters chanting "Better the grave than a slave, better a war than the pact". In the early hours of 27 March a bloodless military <i>coup d'état</i> was executed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERametLazić201118_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERametLazić201118-77">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> In the wake of the coup, the new government refused to ratify Yugoslavia's signing of the Tripartite Pact, but did not openly rule it out.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMilazzo19752–3_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMilazzo19752–3-78">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> The coup found little support with the Croatian population,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchreiberStegemannVogel1995482_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchreiberStegemannVogel1995482-79">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> and on the day after the invasion commenced Maček resigned from the government and returned to Zagreb in anticipation of unrest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001-80">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Invasion_and_establishment_of_the_Independent_State_of_Croatia">Invasion and establishment of the Independent State of Croatia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Invasion and establishment of the Independent State of Croatia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ante_Paveli%C4%87_and_Alojzije_Stepinac.jpg" class="image"><img alt="a male in archbishop&#39;s garb greeting a male in military uniform" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Ante_Paveli%C4%87_and_Alojzije_Stepinac.jpg/220px-Ante_Paveli%C4%87_and_Alojzije_Stepinac.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="265" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Ante_Paveli%C4%87_and_Alojzije_Stepinac.jpg/330px-Ante_Paveli%C4%87_and_Alojzije_Stepinac.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Ante_Paveli%C4%87_and_Alojzije_Stepinac.jpg/440px-Ante_Paveli%C4%87_and_Alojzije_Stepinac.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1511" data-file-height="1818" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ante_Paveli%C4%87_and_Alojzije_Stepinac.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Archbishop Stepinac greeting the fascist Ustaše leader Ante Pavelić</div></div></div>
<p>Hitler was furious when he learned of the coup, and later on 27 March 1941 he ordered the invasion of Yugoslavia. Commencing on 6 April, a German-led Axis invasion force began its assault from multiple directions, quickly overcoming the limited resistance. During the fighting, several Croat units mutinied and others performed poorly or defected. On 10 April 1941, with the assistance of the Germans, the senior Ustaše figure in the country, <a href="/wiki/Slavko_Kvaternik" title="Slavko Kvaternik">Slavko Kvaternik</a>, proclaimed the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Independent_State_of_Croatia" title="Independent State of Croatia">Independent State of Croatia</a> (Croatian: <i lang="hr">Nezavisna Država Hrvatska</i>, NDH). German tanks entered Zagreb later that same day and were greeted by cheering crowds.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEU.S._Army198660_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEU.S._Army198660-81">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Before the war the <a href="/wiki/Usta%C5%A1e" title="Ustaše">Ustaše</a> were a fascist, ultranationalist, racist and terrorist organization, fighting for an independent Croatia. Ustaše terrorists set off bombs on international trains bound for Yugoslavia,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich197533_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich197533-82">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> and were convicted in the 1934 assassination of the Yugoslav King and French foreign Minister in Marseilles.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich197533_82-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich197533-82">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Ante_Paveli%C4%87" title="Ante Pavelić">Ante Pavelić</a>, Kvaternik and other Ustaše leaders were sentenced to death in absentia by French courts, as the true assassination ringleaders.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich197534_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich197534-83">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> The Ustaše "17 Principles" proclaimed that those who were not "of Croat blood" (i.e. Serbs and Jews), will not have any political role in the future Croat state. In his 1936 tract, "The Croat Question", the Ustaše leader, Pavelić, spouted anti-Serb and anti-Semitic hatred, calling Jews the enemy of the Croat people.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 12 April, Stepinac visited Kvaternik and pledged his loyalty to the NDH.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199583_42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199583-42">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> The following day, when the Ustaše leader Ante Pavelić arrived in Zagreb, Stepinac did not participate in the welcome, but he did visit Pavelić on 16 April. These meetings and a radio broadcast all occurred prior to the capitulation of the Yugoslav armed forces on 17 April.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199583_42-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199583-42">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> That evening, Stepinac hosted a dinner party for Pavelić and the leading Ustaše.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECornwell2008253_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECornwell2008253-85">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> On 27 April, Stepinac recorded in his diary that Pavelić assured him he will act per the Catholic Church's desires, and that Pavelić stated he will “exterminate” the <a href="/wiki/Old_Catholic_Church" title="Old Catholic Church">Old Catholic Church</a> (which rejects Papal authority) and will not be tolerant of the Serbian Orthodox Church, because it was not the Church for him, but a political organization. Based on this Stepinac concluded in his diary that Pavelic is "a sincere Catholic and that the Church would enjoy freedom to carry out its work", although he recognised that difficulties lay ahead.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42-86">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> On the same day, the official Croatian Catholic newspaper <i>Nedelja</i> praised both Pavelić and Hitler, saying:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199584_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199584-88">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>God, who directs the destiny of nations and controls the hearts of kings, has given us Ante Pavelić and moved the leader of friendly and allied people, Adolf Hitler, to use his victorious troops to disperse our oppressors and enable us to create an Independent State of Croatia. Glory be to God, our gratitude to Adolf Hitler, and infinite loyalty to our Poglavnik, Ante Pavelić.</p></blockquote>
<p>Immediately the Ustaše implemented draconian decrees specifying death as the sole penalty for those who attempt to act against the regime (April 17),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001383–384_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001383–384-89">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> opened the first <a href="/wiki/Danica_concentration_camp" title="Danica concentration camp">concentration camp</a> (April 15), and initiated the persecution of Jews and Serbs. Aware the Ustaše were preparing Nazi-style Race Laws, on April 23 Stepinac wrote Ustaše interior minister <a href="/wiki/Andrija_Artukovi%C4%87" title="Andrija Artuković">Andrija Artkuković</a> a letter, in which Stepinac calls the Race Laws "necessary", and only asks that Jewish converts to Catholicism be exempted.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Five days later, on 28 April, Stepinac issued a "rapturous" encyclical to his diocese regarding the creation of the "young Croatian state",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42_86-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42-86">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> which included the words:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199584_88-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199584-88">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>Our people has come face to face with its age-old and ardently desired dream. The times are such that it is no longer the tongue which speaks but the blood with its mysterious links with the country, in which we have seen the light of God, and with its people from whom we spring. Do we need to say that the blood flows more quickly in our veins, that the hearts in our breasts beat faster?&#160;... It is easy to see God's hand at work here.</p></blockquote>
<p>Stepinac urged the clergy of his archdiocese to fulfill their duty to the new Croatian state and pray that the head of state, i.e. the Ustashe leader Pavelić,
</p>
<blockquote><p>may have the spirit and wisdom in order to fulfill noble and responsible office for the glory of God and the salvation of the people in truth and justice.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001555_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001555-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Stepinac's letter captured what was a common sentiment among Croatian nationalists and much of the Catholic Church in the new state.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42_86-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42-86">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Considering the marginal role of the Church in the political arena during the interwar period, the creation of the NDH appeared to offer the Church and the Croatian Catholic movement an opportunity. The leaders of the new state appeared willing to work with Church leaders, and thus reduce the marginalisation the Church had been subject to under the Yugoslav state.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a41_50-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a41-50">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stepinac's immediate visits to Kvaternik and Pavelić, and his diocesan letter all assisted the Ustaše in consolidating their control of the new state,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001370_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001370-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> and enhanced its credibility with the Croatian people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETanner1997144_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanner1997144-93">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Cornwell notes that this letter was issued on the same day that <a href="/wiki/Gudovac_massacre" title="Gudovac massacre">nearly 200 Serbs were massacred</a> by the Ustaše near <a href="/wiki/Bjelovar" title="Bjelovar">Bjelovar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECornwell2008253_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECornwell2008253-85">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Even prior to Stepinac's letter, Ustaše and Volksdeutche had already <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.jewishvirtuallibrary.org/osijek">destroyed the Osijek synagogue</a> (14 April), and on 17 April Pavelić had issued the Decree on the Protection of the Nation and the State, the first of the acts that placed Serbs, Jews and Roma outside the law, leading to their persecution and destruction.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001383–384_89-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001383–384-89">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> The Ustaše had opened and started filling <a href="/wiki/Danica_concentration_camp" title="Danica concentration camp">their first concentration camp</a> (15 April), and had instituted additional <a href="/wiki/Ante_Paveli%C4%87#Legislation" title="Ante Pavelić">discriminatory edicts against Jews and Serbs</a> (14, 19, 22 and 25 April). Stepinac already knew of the planned racial laws, which Pavelić signed only 2 days after Stepinac issued his letter praising Pavelić and the Ustaše state.
</p><p>On 30 April 1941, Pavelić signed the main race laws - the <i>Legal Decree on Racial Origins</i>, and the <i>Legal Decree on the Protection of Aryan Blood and the Honor of the Croatian People</i>.  In a letter to the Pope from May 16, Stepinac wrote that the race laws were meant to appease the Nazis, and concludes that "it was a much lesser evil that the Croats passed this law rather than that the Germans took all power into their own hands".<sup id="cite_ref-:0_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-94">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> In a May 14 letter to the Pope, when he knew of the slaughter of 260 Orthodox Serbs held in the Glina Church, Stepinac praised Ustaše efforts to turn Croatia into a "Catholic country", and praised <a href="/wiki/Ante_Paveli%C4%87" title="Ante Pavelić">Pavelić</a> in particular, noting he will "liquidate" (eliminate) Orthodox Serbs from Croatia:
</p>
<blockquote><p>Completely honestly, I notice that in the circles of power there is the utmost desire to turn Croatia into a Catholic country. The Minister of War [<a href="/wiki/Slavko_Kvaternik" title="Slavko Kvaternik">Slavko Kvaternik</a>] absolutely guaranteed me: either Croatia will be a Catholic country, or let it disappear…The desire of those who currently rule Croatia to implement the teachings of the Catholic Church obliges us to help and support them with all the loyalty and strength we have." Stepinac added: "Pavelić is a true Catholic practicing believer, and he wants to create, despite huge obstacles, a Catholic state in Croatia&#160;... I believe that if the Poglavnik, <a href="/wiki/Ante_Paveli%C4%87" title="Ante Pavelić">Pavelić</a>, were head of government for 20 years, the schismatics [i.e. Orthodox Serbs] would be completely liquidated (eliminated) from Croatia." Referencing Nazi plans to expel 260,000 Slovenes, Stepinac wrote: "Earlier [Pavelić] asked me what I thought if he decided to ask the German authorities to change the fate of Slovenes and transfer them as Catholics to Croatia, among Catholics, and transfer the same number of schismatic Serbs from Croatia to Macedonia. I replied to [Pavelić] that such a solution would be better for the unfortunate Slovenes"<sup id="cite_ref-:0_94-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-94">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Croatian historian, Hrvoje Klasić, notes Stepinac supported actions that today's courts would classify as ethnic cleansing of Serbs.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> In May 1941 Pavelić visited the Pope. Phayer writes that Stepinac arranged the audience with Pius XII,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhayer200032_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhayer200032-96">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> and "recommended the dictator to the Holy See".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhayer200037_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhayer200037-97">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Ester Gitman writes that "Stepinac chose not to join Pavelić" and that he was given a private audience with the Pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman200650_25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman200650-25">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Pavelić put pressure on Archbishop Stepinac to write to Pope Pius XII, via Cardinal Maglione, to request official recognition of the Independent State of Croatia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman200650_25-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman200650-25">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The answer came back in July - in accordance with long-standing tradition during wartime, no Vatican recognition of the NDH was forthcoming. But the Pope did send Abbot <a href="/wiki/Giuseppe_Marcone" title="Giuseppe Marcone">Giuseppe Marcone</a> as apostolic visitor, who acted as papal nuncio, which satisfied Stepinac, since he felt "the Vatican had de facto recognized the new state".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhayer200032_96-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhayer200032-96">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 16 report to the Pope, Stepinac wrote:
</p>
<blockquote><p>It is obvious for now, the pressure of the Germans, who are very much felt in the laws against Jews, although they claim that they do not want to interfere in the internal affairs of the Croatian State. Indeed, the racist law passed these days must be attributed to the severe pressures of Germany, because I know from personal encounters with people who run the state that they do not intend to keep the law in full force for long as it has been published. It is much less evil that the Croats passed this law than if the Germans had taken all power into their own hands.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> 
</p></blockquote>
<p>Pavelić met Hitler for the first time on 7 June 1941, and told him that many younger clergy were supportive of the Ustaše regime, but mentioned that Stepinac had advised him that he could only rule if he was "as forebearing as possible". Biondich notes that Stepinac was unhappy that many younger priests were overtly supporting the Ustaše.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a43_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a43-99">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> On 26 June 1941, Stepinac met with the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Vrhbosna" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Vrhbosna">Archbishop of Vrhbosna</a>  and the bishops of <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Belgrade" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Belgrade">Belgrade</a>, <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Banja_Luka" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Banja Luka">Banja Luka</a>, <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Split-Makarska" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Split-Makarska">Split</a>, <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Hvar-Bra%C4%8D-Vis" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Hvar-Brač-Vis">Hvar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_%C5%A0ibenik" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Šibenik">Šibenik</a> and <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Senj-Modru%C5%A1" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Senj-Modruš">Senj-Modruš</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Mostar-Duvno" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Mostar-Duvno">Bishop of Mostar</a> sent a <a href="/wiki/Friar" title="Friar">friar</a> to the meeting. The group decided to go to Pavelić to express their devotion and trust. At the reception with Pavelić, Stepinac stated that "love of religion and country spring only from God", then promised Pavelić their loyalty and co-operation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199588–89_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199588–89-100">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite initially welcoming the Independent State of Croatia, Stepinac subsequently<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers"><span title="When (and how) did he condemn? After or before the fall of the Nazis and Ustaše? (February 2020)">when?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> condemned the Nazi-aligned state's atrocities against Jews and Serbs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGilbert2003236_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGilbert2003236-101">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> He objected to the persecution of Jews and Nazi laws, helped Jews and others to escape and criticized Ustaše atrocities in front of <a href="/wiki/Zagreb_Cathedral" title="Zagreb Cathedral">Zagreb Cathedral</a> in 1943.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJansen200387,_151_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJansen200387,_151-102">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> Despite this, Stepinac never broke with the Ustaše regime and continued to attend public gatherings at their side.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKent2002164_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKent2002164-103">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> After the invasion and Italian annexation of much of the Dalmatian coast, the <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiastical_province" title="Ecclesiastical province">ecclesiastical province</a> of the Zagreb archbishopric included the Archdiocese of Zagreb, as well as the dioceses of <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_%C4%90akovo-Osijek" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Đakovo-Osijek">Đakovo</a> and Senj-Modruš, and the <a href="/wiki/Croatian_Catholic_Eparchy_of_Kri%C5%BEevci" class="mw-redirect" title="Croatian Catholic Eparchy of Križevci">Greek Catholic Bishopric of Križevci</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a58_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a58-104">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac had very limited formal authority over the <a href="/wiki/Suffragan_bishop" title="Suffragan bishop">suffragan bishops</a> of his province, being more of a "first among equals" than a superior. Biondich states that Stepinac did not have the power to dictate policy or control the behaviour of the Sarajevo-based Archbishop of Vrhbosna, Ivan Šarić, or the other bishops in the NDH.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42_86-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42-86">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Yet Goldstein notes that Stepinac communicated with Sarajevo Archbishop Ivan Šarić, but apparently never even privately condemned his public support for the Ustaše genocides, unlike the <a href="/wiki/Croatian_Peasant_Party" title="Croatian Peasant Party">Croatian Peasant Party</a> members in London who vehemently condemned Šarić.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018585_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018585-105">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By the end of Summer 1941, Ante Pavelić publicly blamed the irregular "Wild Ustashe" (some 25,000-30,000 of them, including many criminal elements) for all the atrocities performed by the Ustashe in the previous months. The state authorities never paid members of "Wild Ustashe", nor acknowledged them to be in the military service, though government-controlled press and Ustashe officials in former months clearly encouraged those aggressive but undisciplined groups to go against the "enemy elements", as the regime needed the manpower to execute ethnic cleansing and genocide within the NDH. Some members of that irregular part of the <a href="/wiki/Ustashe_Militia" class="mw-redirect" title="Ustashe Militia">Ustashe Militia</a> were arrested, tried and executed for the crimes against Serbs and Gypsies. This was done to convey a message to the rest of the "Wild Ustashe" to become part of the Ustashe hierarchy through regimentation.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Persecution against Serbs, Jews and Roma persisted, however.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relations_with_the_government">Relations with the government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Relations with the government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The historian, Jozo Tomasevich, states that the NDH regime unleashed genocidal policies against Jews, Serbs and Roma, while also subjecting Croats to the greatest repression they experienced in their history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001381_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001381-107">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/John_Van_Antwerp_Fine_Jr." title="John Van Antwerp Fine Jr.">John Fine</a>, Stepinac enjoyed close associations with the Ustaše leaders, as he was the archbishop of the capital.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> During the war Stepinac personally celebrated <a href="/wiki/Te_Deum" title="Te Deum">Te Deums</a> on the anniversary of the founding of the NDH, including on April 10., 1945.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-108">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac served as the <a href="/wiki/Military_ordinariate" title="Military ordinariate">military Vicar</a> of the NDH army throughout the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> He appeared in photographs numerous times in Ustaše newspapers, alongside Pavelić and other Ustaše leaders.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_108-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-108">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582–583_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582–583-110">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Although he later sought to distance himself from the Ustaše,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001372_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001372-111">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> especially when it became clear the Axis powers and the Ustaše would lose the war,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001556_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001556-112">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac and the hierarchy of Catholic Church in Croatia nevertheless continued to publicly support the Ustaše regime until the very end.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001372_111-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001372-111">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In mid-May 1941, Maglione was already noting that Stepinac and other bishops were treading cautiously with the NDH authorities to avoid "compromising themselves" with the Ustaše leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a43_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a43-99">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> In July 1941, no <a href="/wiki/Te_Deum" title="Te Deum">Te Deum</a> was sung at the <a href="/wiki/Zagreb_Cathedral" title="Zagreb Cathedral">Zagreb Cathedral</a> in celebration of Pavelić's birthday, which contributed to tension between Stepinac and the Ustaše leader.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman200651_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman200651-113">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Yet that same month, at the end of the first NDH Bishop's conference, the bishops were received by Pavelić. Stepinac personally greeted Pavelić and stated that the bishops were visiting him <i>"as the legitimate representatives of the Church of God in the NDH, with the promise of sincere and loyal cooperation for a better future of our&#160;homeland"</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> In October 1941, shortly after the Ustaše destroyed the main <a href="/wiki/Zagreb_Synagogue" title="Zagreb Synagogue">synagogue in Zagreb</a>, Stepinac preached a sermon in which he said,
</p>
<blockquote><p>"A House of God, of whatever religion, is a holy place. Whoever touches such a place will pay with his life. An attack on a House of God of any religion constitutes an attack on all religious communities."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman200656_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman200656-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> (Ivo Goldstein notes that claims that Stepinac said this come from a single individual, and are not confirmed by any other source, despite the fact Stepinac supposedly made the speech in the Zagreb cathedral. Goldstein also notes it's contrary to the rather gentler criticism of Ustaše race laws in well-documented, private-only communications at that time)<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In November 1941, Stepinac chaired a bishop's conference, during which he heard reports from various bishops within the NDH. What he heard made his enthusiasm wane for the new Croatian state. On 20 November he wrote to Pavelić including some of the reports he had received. He stated that he believed that the worst of the atrocities were over, and that he believed they were the work of individuals. Yet contrary to Stepinac blaming individuals, historians state the Ustaše regime carried out systematic genocidal policies against Jews, Serbs and Roma.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001381_107-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001381-107">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> The letter did challenge Pavelić, stating that "no-one can deny that these terrible acts of violence and cruelty have been taking place", pointing out that Pavelić himself had condemned the atrocities committed by the Ustaše. He said, "The Croatian nation has been proud of its 1000-year-old culture and Christian tradition. That is why we wait for it to show in practice, now that it has achieved its freedom, a greater nobility and humanity than that displayed by its former rulers".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETanner1997155_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanner1997155-117">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> Tomasevich notes that Stepinac never made this letter public, e.g. in the form of a pastoral letter, which could have had a beneficial effect on restraining Ustaše crimes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001540_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001540-118">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> In fact Tomasevich writes that neither Stepinac, nor anyone else in the Church hierarchy ever uttered one word of public protest against Ustaše crimes against Serbs, by far the most numerous victims of NDH genocidal policies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001537_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001537-119">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 1941, Pavelić met with the <a href="/wiki/Italian_Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs" title="Italian Minister of Foreign Affairs">Italian foreign minister</a>, Count <a href="/wiki/Galeazzo_Ciano" title="Galeazzo Ciano">Galeazzo Ciano</a>, and told him that the lower levels of Catholic clergy displayed a very positive attitude towards the Ustaše regime, but that some of the bishops were openly hostile to the government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a43–44_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a43–44-120">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Also in December, Stepinac declared membership in <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Action" title="Catholic Action">Catholic Action</a> and in the Ustaše to be incompatible.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Cornwell states that Stepinac was "wholly in accord with the general goals of the new Croatian state".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECornwell2008253_85-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECornwell2008253-85">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Tomasevich states that the Catholic Church fully supported Ustaše policies, and failed to publicly condemn crimes against Serbs. Many priests and well-known Catholic laymen openly sided with the Ustaše, assumed responsible positions in the NDH, while the Catholic press praised the Pavelić regime.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001555–556_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001555–556-122">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> On more than one occasion, the archbishop proclaimed his support for the Independent State of Croatia and welcomed the demise of Yugoslavia<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285_1-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>  On 10 April each year during the war he celebrated a mass to celebrate proclamation of the NDH.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander1987_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander1987-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2015)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In June 1945 the Papal legate to the NDH, Marcone, related to a British officer in Zagreb that the Croatian Catholic Church during the war “tended to identify itself too closely with the Ustaše“, because (1) many Croat priests were passionate believers in Croatia's independence, thus they were tempted to turn a blind eye on Ustaše atrocities, and (2) they were drawn to leading Ustaše because they were devout Catholics, including the worst criminals like Pavelić, Rukavina and Luburić.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001555–556_122-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001555–556-122">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May, 1943, Stepinac sent a report to the Papal secretary of state, on the attitude of the Catholic Church in Croatia towards the Ustaše regime.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001371_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001371-123">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Responding to criticisms that the Church had not done enough to oppose Ustaše crimes, Stepinac enumerated the main benefits the Church received from the Ustaše regime: it fights abortion, which Stepinac claimed is mainly performed by Jewish and Serb doctors, and has banned pornography, according to Stepinac, published by Jews and Serbs; that it abolished Freemasonry and vigorously fought against communism, that it issued decrees against swearing. The Ustaše government ensured the Christian education of NDH soldiers; insisted on religious education in schools; that it increased financial aid to Catholic religious institutions, increased the salaries of the clergy, supported the Church's charitable activities, financially supported the construction of new and the repair of existing churches. According to Stepinac, all these were indicators of the Ustaše regime's goodwill towards the Catholic Church, and “as the Archbishop's report makes clear, the Church reciprocated in kind.”<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001371_123-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001371-123">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the same letter Stepinac described Yugoslav government-in-exile complaints against the Ustaše regime as "enemy propaganda" aimed at bringing the NDH into disrepute in the eyes of the Vatican. He admitted that atrocities had been committed against Serbs by irresponsible people without the sanction of the NDH authorities, and claimed that many of those responsible had been executed by the government. He deplored and condemned the atrocities, but stated that they were a reaction to Serb behavior during the interwar period during which, he claimed, Serbs had violated all the rights of the Croatian people. He also reminded the Cardinal of the assassination of the Croatian deputies in the Parliament in 1928.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001371–372_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001371–372-124">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> Tomasevich notes the Ustaše also sought to justify, what they regularly referred to as “some excesses” by individual Ustaše and “wild Ustaše”, by invoking prewar repressive acts by the Serb-dominated Yugoslav regime.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001402_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001402-125">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> However, contrary to Stepinac's views, Tomasevich writes that “the wartime Ustaše policies against the Serbs were of a genocidal nature and totally out of proportion to earlier [Serb] anti-Croatian measures, both in nature and extent”. Furthermore, the Ustaše regime also carried genocides against Jews and Roma,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001404_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001404-126">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> in NDH state-run concentration camps.
</p><p>Pavelić attended services at Zagreb Cathedral only once in the four years he was in power, and Stepinac did not greet him at the entrance on that occasion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman200651_113-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman200651-113">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac lost control of the Archdiocese's publication <i>Katolički List</i> under the new regime.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman2011101_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman2011101-127">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> In 1942, officials from <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary_(1920%E2%80%931946)" title="Kingdom of Hungary (1920–1946)">Hungary</a> lobbied to ecclesiastically attach <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_occupation_of_Yugoslav_territories" title="Hungarian occupation of Yugoslav territories">Hungarian-occupied Međimurje</a> to a diocese in Hungary. Stepinac opposed this and received guarantees from the Holy See that diocesan boundaries would not change during the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKolanović1998_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKolanović1998-128">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2015)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> On 26 October 1943, the Germans killed the archbishop's brother Mijo.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHorvat1996_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHorvat1996-129">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2015)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>According to Tanner, Stepinac remained naive about politics and the nature of the Ustaše regime. In 1943, Stepinac travelled to the Vatican and came into contact with the Croatian artist <a href="/wiki/Ivan_Me%C5%A1trovi%C4%87" title="Ivan Meštrović">Ivan Meštrović</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Gitman,_Elise_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gitman,_Elise-130">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> According to Meštrović, Stepinac asked him whether he thought Pavelić knew about the killings of Serbs. When Meštrović replied that Pavelić must know everything, Stepinac went pale and burst into tears.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETanner1997155–156_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanner1997155–156-131">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The historian <a href="/wiki/Martin_Gilbert" title="Martin Gilbert">Martin Gilbert</a> wrote that despite initially welcoming the Independent State of Croatia, Stepinac later "condemned Croat atrocities against both Serbs and Jews, and himself saved a group of Jews in an old age home".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGilbert2003236_101-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGilbert2003236-101">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> According to West, Stepinac and the entire Catholic Church remained loyal to Pavelić and the NDH.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199574–75_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199574–75-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> West states that Stepinac was one of the priests and father-confessors to senior Ustaše such as Pavelić, Budak, Kvaternik and Artuković.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest199582_27-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest199582-27">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:NDH_-_salute.jpg" class="image"><img alt="three priests and several saluting males in military uniform" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/NDH_-_salute.jpg/220px-NDH_-_salute.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="181" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/NDH_-_salute.jpg/330px-NDH_-_salute.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/NDH_-_salute.jpg/440px-NDH_-_salute.jpg 2x" data-file-width="540" data-file-height="445" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:NDH_-_salute.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Stepinac (far right) with two Catholic priests at the funeral of President of the <a href="/wiki/Croatian_Parliament" title="Croatian Parliament">Croatian Parliament</a> <a href="/wiki/Marko_Do%C5%A1en" title="Marko Došen">Marko Došen</a> in September 1944</div></div></div>
<p>In 1944, the NDH Ministry for Justice and Religion proposed, and Stepinac accepted the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Order_of_Merit_(Croatia)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Order of Merit (Croatia) (page does not exist)">Order of Merit</a> medal from Pavelić,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECvitković1986170_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECvitković1986170-133">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> for "having as archbishop unmasked inside and outside the country the opponents of the Independent State of Croatia"<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Catholic Church in the NDH then began to criticise actions taken by the government, and attempted to distance itself to some extent from the authorities. It had no real alternative, given that the likely alternate governments were led by Serb-chauvinist <a href="/wiki/Chetniks" title="Chetniks">Chetniks</a> or communists. By that time the Ustaše had long lost the support of the great majority of  the Croatian people, and most members of the main Croatian prewar party, the <a href="/wiki/Croatian_Peasant_Party" title="Croatian Peasant Party">Croatian Peasant Party</a>, were supporting the Partisans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001367_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001367-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> Contrary to this, the Church maintained its support of the NDH government to the bitter end.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001372_111-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001372-111">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> This is demonstrated by the pastoral letter issued after the episcopal conference of 24 March 1945, in which the Croatian Catholic Church maintained its formal support for the puppet state and its rulers, despite the fact that most senior <a href="/wiki/Regime" title="Regime">regime</a> figures were preparing to flee the country.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001550_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001550-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> The Episcopal Conference was convened at the urging of the Ustaše government, who also had a hand in drafting the letter, issued after the formation of the new, unified Yugoslav government, which had already been recognized by the Allies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001550_136-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001550-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> According to a later British representative in Zagreb, the papal legate Marcone stated that if he had known of the Bishops' letter of support for the NDH, he would have done everything to prevent its publication<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001550_136-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001550-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> (during the war the Vatican recognized the Yugoslav government in exile, and postwar recognized the new Yugoslav government). The Catholic press in the NDH also maintained its support of Pavelić right to the end.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001370_92-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001370-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ivo Goldstein notes Stepinac's protests against Ustaše crimes, yet states that Stepinac's biggest failure was his public support for the criminal NDH, from its first dayt to its last - he celebrated the coming of the Ustaše to power, held Te Deums on the anniversaries of the NDH (even on April 10, 1945), on numerous occasions he was photographed with Pavelić and other Ustaše officials, all of which provided legitimacy to Ustaše regime, allowing it to maintain power and commit crimes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582–583_110-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582–583-110">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> The Ustaše ignored Stepinac's private criticisms, while only a relatively small number of people heard his few public criticisms. On the other hand, countless people saw and read about Stepinac's public support for the NDH. Furthermore, Stepinac expressed much of his criticisms only after the Ustaše had already committed most of their genocidal crimes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582–583_110-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582–583-110">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Biondich concludes that claims that Stepinac was an Ustaše sympathiser, and even the spiritual leader of the regime are unfounded. He further states that while Stepinac supported independence, he "began privately to distance himself from the regime within weeks, and certainly within months of the Croatian state's formation." He also observes that while Stepinac continued to attend to his ceremonial duties at official state events, he was privately raising his concerns with the Ustaše leaders.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a44_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a44-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> However Biondich also states that Stepinac was not the outspoken critic of the Ustaše regime that many of his defenders claim.
</p><p>On the other hand, historian Robert McCormick states, "for all the Archbishop's hand wringing, he continued to be a tacit participant in the <a href="/wiki/Independent_State_of_Croatia" title="Independent State of Croatia">Independent State of Croatia</a> (ISC). He repeatedly appeared in public with the Poglavnik (the Ustaše leader <a href="/wiki/Ante_Paveli%C4%87" title="Ante Pavelić">Ante Pavelić</a>), and issued Te Deum's on the anniversary of the NDH's creation. His failure to publicly denounce the Ustaše's atrocities in the name of the NDH, was tantamount to accepting Pavelić's policies".<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 10 April 1945, Stepinac held a mass in the Zagreb Cathedral for the 4th anniversary of the NDH's founding, and Te Deum's were sung for what was left of the Ustaše state.<sup id="cite_ref-Yugoslavia,_Richard_West_2009,_p.161_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Yugoslavia,_Richard_West_2009,_p.161-139">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Richard_West_(journalist)" title="Richard West (journalist)">Richard West</a> writes that, on 15 April, as Pavelić and other Ustaše leaders were getting ready to flee, "Archbishop Stepinac devoted his sermon to what he believed was Croatia's worst sin, not mass murder, but swearing."<sup id="cite_ref-Yugoslavia,_Richard_West_2009,_p.161_139-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Yugoslavia,_Richard_West_2009,_p.161-139">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Response_to_Usta.C5.A1e_atrocities"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Response_to_Ustaše_atrocities">Response to Ustaše atrocities</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Response to Ustaše atrocities">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"/><table class="box-Essay-like plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-style ambox-essay-like" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/f/f2/Edit-clear.svg/40px-Edit-clear.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="40" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/f/f2/Edit-clear.svg/60px-Edit-clear.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/f/f2/Edit-clear.svg/80px-Edit-clear.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="48" data-file-height="48" /></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>is written like a <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:What_Wikipedia_is_not#Wikipedia_is_not_a_publisher_of_original_thought" title="Wikipedia:What Wikipedia is not">personal reflection, personal essay, or argumentative essay</a></b> that states a Wikipedia editor's personal feelings or presents an original argument about a topic.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit">help improve it</a> by rewriting it in an <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Writing_better_articles#Information_style_and_tone" title="Wikipedia:Writing better articles">encyclopedic style</a>.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">August 2018</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>The atrocities committed by the Ustaše can be categorised into four broad areas, all of which fell largely on the Serb population of the NDH; racial laws, mass killings and concentration camps, deportations, and forced conversions to Catholicism.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Racial_laws">Racial laws</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Racial laws">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>On 23 April 1941, Stepinac wrote to the Ustaše Interior Minister, Artuković, "on the occasion of the announced passage of anti-Jewish laws", to caution of "good Catholics who are of the Jewish race and who have converted from the Jewish religion&#160;... I consider that it would be necessary, in passing the necessary laws, to take converts of this kind into account."<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Historian Ivo Goldstein notes that Stepinac not only did not protest against the adoption of racial laws, he called them "necessary", and only asked that Jewish converts to Catholicism be exempted.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Following Pavelić's proclamation of the Race Laws, Stepinac praised Pavelić to the Pope, as "a true Catholic practicing believer",<sup id="cite_ref-:0_94-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-94">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> and stated that "it was a much lesser evil that the Croats passed this law rather than that the Germans took all power into their own hands".<sup id="cite_ref-:0_94-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-94">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stepinac again wrote to Artuković on 22 May to protest the race laws and their application to converted Jews, telling him that members of other races should not be discriminated against "through no fault of their own."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42_86-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42-86">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> He wrote, "We&#160;... appeal to you to issue regulations so that even in the framework of antisemitic legislation, and similar legislation concerning Serbs, the principles of human dignity be preserved."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhayer200035_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhayer200035-142">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac added: "Everyone will certainly approve the attempt for the economy to be in national hands, not to allow a non-national and anti-national element to amass capital, or foreign elements to decide about the State and the people. But to take away any possibility of existence from members of other peoples or other races, and to brand them with the stamp of shame, this is an issue of humanity and a question of morality".<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Goldstein notes, Stepinac seemingly argued for "humane" race laws. On 24 May 1942, Stepinac condemned racial persecution in general terms, although he did not  mention <a href="/wiki/Serbs" title="Serbs">Serbs</a>. He stated in a diocesan letter:
</p>
<blockquote><p>All men and all races are children of God; all without distinction. Those who are Gypsies, Black, European, or Aryan all have the same rights&#160;... for this reason, the Catholic Church had always condemned, and continues to condemn, all injustice and all violence committed in the name of theories of class, race, or nationality. It is not permissible to persecute Gypsies or Jews because they are thought to be an inferior race.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJansen2003151_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJansen2003151-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldhagen2002146_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldhagen2002146-145">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In a sermon on 25 October 1942, he further commented on racial acceptance:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBreitman2005208_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBreitman2005208-146">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>We affirm then that all peoples and races descend from God. In fact, there exists but one race&#160;... The members of this race can be white or black, they can be separated by oceans or live on the opposing poles, [but] they remain first and foremost the race created by God, according to the precepts of natural law and positive Divine law as it is written in the hearts and minds of humans or revealed by Jesus Christ, the son of God, the sovereign of all peoples.</p></blockquote>
<p>In response to criticisms by the Yugoslav government in exile, that the Church had not done enough to counter Ustaše crimes, in May 1943 Stepinac wrote a letter to the Papal secretary in which he acknowledged the crimes, yet praised the Ustaše, among other reasons, for fighting abortion and pornography, with Stepinac blaming Jews and Serbs for both.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001371_123-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001371-123">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his homily of 31 October 1943, which some claim is his most resolute critique of the Ustaše,<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac first inveighed against abortion, the "pagan fashions of today's female world" and "all the licentiousness&#160;... that has been observed&#160;... at sea beaches and other bathing spots". He blames these "sins" for the fact that "God like thunder today brings down not just cities and villages, but entire peoples". The speech's primary theme is the defense of the Church's actions, against those who "accuse us of not having arisen in timely or appropriate fashion against the crimes in parts of our homeland". Stepinac states that "the Church cannot force others to behave according to God's laws" and cannot be responsible for "the hotheads in its own priestly ranks". He proclaims it was not the Church that "created in the souls of people the dissatisfaction and insatiability which has produced such sad consequences", instead he blames "certain circles, organizations, and members of other national groups", which  some sources state is a reference to Serbs and perhaps Jews.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-148">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stepinac then criticizes Communism, its denial of private property rights, its approval of divorce, negation of God, refusal to allow religious education in schools, etc. Finally at the end of the homily he states: "The Catholic Church does not know races that lord over others, or slave races. The Catholic Church knows only races and peoples as the creatures of God, and if it values some more, it is those of noble heart, and not of stronger fist. For her, a king in the royal palace is a man, in the same way as the last pauper and gypsy under a tent&#160;... The system of shooting hundreds of hostages for a crime in which no culprit can be found is a pagan system that never yielded a good fruit".<sup id="cite_ref-:2_148-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-148">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In this speech Stepinac condemns "all the wrongdoing, all the killing of the innocent, all the burning of villages". Much of his public criticism was spoken after most of the genocides were already completed, and it became clear the Nazis and Ustaše would lose. These belated speeches were made before limited audiences, unlike his pastoral letter, condemning the Communists, that he ordered read from all the pulpits across Croatia, only 4 months after the Communists seized power. In a letter to the Vatican of May 1943, Stepinac still praised the Ustaše for the "good things" they had done, including the "strict ban on all pornographic publications, which were first and foremost published by Jews and Orthodox!".<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stepinac was involved directly and indirectly in efforts to save Jews from persecution. <a href="/wiki/Amiel_Shomrony" title="Amiel Shomrony">Amiel Shomrony</a> (Emil Schwartz), was the personal secretary of <a href="/wiki/Miroslav_%C5%A0alom_Freiberger" title="Miroslav Šalom Freiberger">Miroslav Šalom Freiberger</a> (the chief <a href="/wiki/Rabbi" title="Rabbi">rabbi</a> in <a href="/wiki/Zagreb" title="Zagreb">Zagreb</a>) until 1942. In the actions for saving Jews, Shomrony acted as the mediator between the chief rabbi and Stepinac. He later stated that he considered Stepinac "truly blessed" since he did the best he could for the Jews during the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman200656_115-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman200656-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Allegedly the Ustaša government at this point agitated at the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy See</a> for him to be removed from the position of archbishop of Zagreb, this however was refused due to the fact that the Vatican did not recognize the Ustaše state (despite Italian pressure).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJansen2003152_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJansen2003152-150">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stepinac and the papal nuncio to <a href="/wiki/Belgrade" title="Belgrade">Belgrade</a> mediated with Royal Italian, Hungarian and Bulgarian troops, urging that the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia" title="Kingdom of Yugoslavia">Yugoslav</a> Jews be allowed to take refuge in the occupied Balkan territories to avoid deportation. He also arranged for Jews to travel via these territories to the safe, neutral states of <a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkey</a> and <a href="/wiki/Spain_under_Franco" class="mw-redirect" title="Spain under Franco">Spain</a>, along with <a href="/wiki/Istanbul" title="Istanbul">Istanbul</a>-based nuncio <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_XXIII" title="Pope John XXIII">Angelo Roncalli</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJansen200387_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJansen200387-151">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> He sent some Jews for safety to Rev Dragutin Jeish, who was killed during the war by the Ustaše on suspicion of supporting the Partisans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECroatian_Ministry_of_Science,_Education_and_Sports_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECroatian_Ministry_of_Science,_Education_and_Sports-152">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Mass_killings_and_concentration_camps">Mass killings and concentration camps</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Mass killings and concentration camps">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Serb_family_killed_in_their_home,_1941.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Serb_family_killed_in_their_home%2C_1941.jpg/220px-Serb_family_killed_in_their_home%2C_1941.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="158" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Serb_family_killed_in_their_home%2C_1941.jpg/330px-Serb_family_killed_in_their_home%2C_1941.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Serb_family_killed_in_their_home%2C_1941.jpg/440px-Serb_family_killed_in_their_home%2C_1941.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1400" data-file-height="1004" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Serb_family_killed_in_their_home,_1941.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A Serb family massacred in their home by the Ustaše in 1941</div></div></div>
<p>The Ustaše unleashed a reign of terror in which 80% of the Jews (30,000 victims) in the NDH were killed, as well as practically all the Roma (25,000 victims), and almost 20% of the Serb population (340,000 victims – see: <a href="/wiki/The_Holocaust_in_the_Independent_State_of_Croatia#Number_of_victims" title="The Holocaust in the Independent State of Croatia">Number of victims</a>). As a military vicar, Stepinac dispensed blessings to the Ustaše armies.<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac initially responded to these mass killings with private letters of protest. Thus, on 14 May 1941, Stepinac received word of an <a href="/wiki/Glina_massacres" title="Glina massacres">Ustaše massacre of Serb villagers at Glina</a>. On the same day, he wrote to Pavelić saying:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42–43_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a42–43-154">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>Just now I received news that the Ustaše in Glina executed without trial and investigation 260 Serbs. I know that the Serbs committed some major crimes in our homeland in these last twenty years. But I consider it my bishop's responsibility to raise my voice and to say that this is not permitted according to Catholic teaching, which is why I ask that you undertake the most urgent measures on the entire territory of the Independent State of Croatia, so that not a single Serb is killed unless it is shown that he committed a crime warranting death. Otherwise, we will not be able to count on the blessing of heaven, without which we must perish.</p></blockquote>
<p>According to Biondich, in the first weeks or even months after the establishment of the NDH, Stepinac may have not known that the atrocities perpetrated by the Ustaše were a key component of their plan. This view supposes that Stepinac considered the atrocities were either spontaneous or the result of so-called "irresponsible elements" who would be held to account by the authorities. His correspondence with Pavelić tends to suggest he did not believe that the <a href="/wiki/Poglavnik" title="Poglavnik">Poglavnik</a> would have sanctioned such actions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a43_99-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a43-99">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Although Stepinac was firmly opposed to the idea of Ustaše to establish a concentration camp within the borders of the Đakovo Bishop's domain, the <a href="/wiki/%C4%90akovo_concentration_camp" class="mw-redirect" title="Đakovo concentration camp">Đakovo concentration camp</a> was established in the deserted flour mill owned by the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_%C4%90akovo-Osijek" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Đakovo-Osijek">Diocese of Đakovo</a> on 1 December 1941.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the other hand, Phayer writes, "it is impossible to believe that Stepinac and the Vatican did not know that the Ustasha murders amounted to genocide", given the "repression and terrorism of the Ustasha regime were without parallel in the history of Southeastern Europe".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhayer200038_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhayer200038-156">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac clearly knew of the May 1941 Glina massacre, and early on that the Ustaše state was sending Jews, Serbs and others to concentration camps. Initially, Stepinac did not oppose these deportations of Jews and others to concentration camps as a matter of principle, but pleaded they be carried out "humanely", by proposing to Pavelić, on 21 July 1941, the introduction of "some particulars to mitigate the procedure: a) for people to be sent to camp in such a way to allow them to prepare what would be the most essential, to allow them to arrange their most urgent obligations both to their families and their jobs; b) for transport not to be in crowded sealed railway cars, especially to distant places; c) to give internees enough food; d) to provide those who are ill with medical treatment; e) to allow the most necessary food to be sent to them, and enable them to correspond with their families.".<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the release of left-wing activist Ante Ciliga from Jasenovac in January 1943, Stepinac requested a meeting with him to learn about what was occurring at the camp.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDujmović1991104–105_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDujmović1991104–105-158">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> After seven Slovene priests were killed at Jasenovac, Stepinac wrote to Pavelić on 24 February 1943, saying:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001400_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001400-159">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>This is a shameful blot and crime which cries to heaven for revenge, as the whole Jasenovac camp is a shameful fault for the Independent State of Croatia&#160;... the entire public, and especially the relatives of the killed priests, ask for compensation and satisfaction and ask that the killers, who are the greatest misfortune for Croatia, be brought before a court of justice.</p></blockquote>
<p>These were still private protest letters. Although later in 1942 and 1943, Stepinac started to speak out more openly against the Ustaše genocides, this was after most of the genocides were already committed, and it became increasingly clear the Nazis and Ustaše will be defeated. Additionally, Stepinac never publicly condemned the numerically greatest Ustaše genocide, that against the Serbs,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001555_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001555-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> and Stepinac continued to support until the very end the Ustaše state that had committed these crimes. Addressing defenders of Stepinac's more private approach, Ivo Goldstein notes that "relatively more persecuted Jews were saved when high church dignitaries openly, energetically and in a principled manner opposed this persecution – as in France (especially the Archbishops of Toulouse and Lyon), the Netherlands, Italy and Denmark, and for a time even in Germany itself. Catholic bishops in Slovakia strongly opposed Nazi demands for radical deportations and thus saved the lives of many converted Jews, and the Orthodox Metropolitan Stefan in Sofia, by tireless public defense of Jewish lives, played a fundamental role in preventing even one Jew from being deported to the Nazi camps from Bulgaria.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>".
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Deportations">Deportations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Deportations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Stepinac again wrote to Pavelić on 21 July 1941 in the wake of mass deportations of Jews and Serbs to concentration camps, stating he was sure that Pavelić was not aware of the atrocities, and that others might not be willing to tell him about them. He wrote that this situation meant there was an even greater obligation on Stepinac to bring them to Pavelić's attention. Referring to Jews as "non-Aryans", Stepinac wrote: "I hear from many quarters that non-Aryans are being treated inhumanely and cruelly during deportations to concentration camps, and in the camps themselves; moreover, that children, the elderly and the sick are not exempt from such treatment." Stepinac urged Pavelić that some things be undertaken "in the direction of easing the procedure: a) that they be sent to the camps in such a way that they be able to prepare the most necessary things, that they can arrange their most urgent obligations to family and service; b) that dispatch is not carried out in overcrowded sealed wagons, especially not to remote places; c) that internees are provided with sufficient food; d) that patients are provided with medical care; e) to allow the dispatch of essential foodstuffs and to facilitate correspondence with families."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018572_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018572-161">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Having heard that some of the deportees were recent converts to Catholicism, he had a duty to show greater concern regarding them. He asked that "humane and Christian consideration&#160;... be shown especially to weak old people, young and innocent children, and the sick." According to Biondich, it is highly likely that Stepinac shared these concerns with the Vatican.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a43_99-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a43-99">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Goldstein, on the other hand, notes that in his letter to Pavelić, Stepinac did not question the deportation of Jews to concentration camps, instead only urged that these be carried out more "humanely".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018572_161-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018572-161">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When deportation of Croatian Jews began, Stepinac and the papal envoy <a href="/wiki/Giuseppe_Marcone" title="Giuseppe Marcone">Giuseppe Marcone</a> protested to <a href="/wiki/Andrija_Artukovic" class="mw-redirect" title="Andrija Artukovic">Andrija Artukovic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhayer200085_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhayer200085-162">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Pope <a href="/wiki/Pius_XII" class="mw-redirect" title="Pius XII">Pius XII</a> had dispatched Marcone as Apostolic Visitor to Croatia, reportedly in order to assist Stepinac and the Croatian Episcopate in "combating the evil influence of neo-pagan propaganda which could be exercised in the organization of the new state".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPreziosi2011_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPreziosi2011-163">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> Marcone served as Nuncio in all but name.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhayer200032_96-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhayer200032-96">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> Mancone reported to Rome on the deteriorating conditions for Croatian Jews, made representations on behalf of the Jews to Croatian officials, and transported Jewish children to safety in neutral Turkey.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Forced_conversions">Forced conversions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Forced conversions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In a circular letter to his clergy, Stepinac initially insisted that conversion had to be done freely, and only after religious instruction. While this and subsequent regulations were designed to protect "the church hierarchy against charges of promoting forced conversions", they also indicated "the church was willing to cooperate with regime's forced conversions, provided the canonical rules were followed".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001539_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001539-164">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> On 3 December 1941, Stepinac sent the pope a report, wherein he notes "the best prospects exist for the conversions".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001541_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001541-165">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, the church's instructions were ignored by the Ustaše authorities. The authorities not only conducted forcible conversions, but on occasion they used the prospect of conversion as a means to gather Serbs together so they could kill them, which is what occurred at Glina. Some Serbs demanded that the local Catholic clergy convert them in order to save their lives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETanner1997151_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanner1997151-166">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Later Stepinac advised individual priests to admit Orthodox believers to the Catholic Church if their lives were in danger, such that this conversion had no validity, allowing them to return to their faith once the danger passed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKrešić200794_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKrešić200794-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 18 May 1943, Stepinac wrote a letter to the pope, in which he estimated 240.000 conversions to date (despite some disputes Tomasevich states this letter is authentic<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001541_165-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001541-165">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>). The Catholic Church in Croatia has also had to contend with criticism of what some have seen as a passive stance towards the Ustaša policy of <a href="/wiki/Religious_conversion" title="Religious conversion">religious conversion</a> whereby some Serbs – but not the intelligentsia element – were able to escape other persecution by adopting the Catholic faith.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECornwell2008253_85-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECornwell2008253-85">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> According to Cornwell, through his role in the forced conversions, Stepinac displayed a "moral dislocation" that "endorsed a contempt for religious freedom tantamount to complicity with the violence".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECornwell2008255_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECornwell2008255-167">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While Stepinac did suspend a number of priests, including Ivo Guberina and Zvonko Brekalo, he only had the authority to do so within his own diocese; he had no power to suspend other priests or bishops outside of Zagreb, as that power was reserved for the Vatican.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhayer200086_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhayer200086-168">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> Due to the arbitrary nature of justice in the NDH and the absence of proper systems for complaint and redress, people such as Stepinac developed an approach of intervening personally with senior government figures on behalf of victims.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001350_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001350-169">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_crimes_against_the_Serbian_Orthodox_Church">Other crimes against the Serbian Orthodox Church</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Other crimes against the Serbian Orthodox Church">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
