<p>In addition to forced conversions, Tomasevich describes other elements of the "Ustaše massive attack against the Serbian Orthodox Church".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001528_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001528-170">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> The Ustaše killed 157 Orthodox priests, among them 3 Serb Orthodox bishops (cutting the throat of the bishop of Banja Luka and killing the archbishop of Sarajevo),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001528_170-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001528-170">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> while they jailed and tortured the Orthodox archbishop of Zagreb, <a href="/wiki/Dositej_Vasi%C4%87" title="Dositej Vasić">Dositej Vasić</a>. The Ustaše expelled to Serbia 327 Orthodox priests and one bishop, while 2 other bishops and 12 priests left on their own.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001529_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001529-171">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Thus 85% of the Orthodox priests in the Independent State of Croatia were either killed or expelled by the Ustaše, in order to "leave the Orthodox population without spiritual leadership so the Ustašas' policy of forced or fear-induced conversions to Catholicism would be easier to carry out".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001529_171-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001529-171">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Ustaše destroyed and desecrated numerous Orthodox Churches,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001529_171-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001529-171">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> forbade the Cyrillic script and Julian calendar (both used in the Orthodox Church), even prohibited the term "Serbian Orthodox Church". Orthodox schools were shut down,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> and the Church was prohibited from collecting contributions from believers, robbing it of income.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531_172-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> Orthodox Church properties were confiscated by the Ustaše,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531_172-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> some turned over to the Croatian Catholic Church. Finally, to destroy the Serbian Orthodox Church, the Ustaše tried to create its own, alternative Croatian Orthodox Church, with an imported Russian priest. but failed to gain adherents.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001545_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001545-173">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tomasevich states that this massive Ustaše attack on the Serbian Orthodox Church "was approved and supported by many Croatian Catholic priests",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531_172-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> and that the Croatian Roman Catholic Church hierarchy and the Vatican "regarded Ustaše policies against the Serbs and Serbian Orthodox Church as advantageous to Roman Catholicism".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001565_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001565-174">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Overall_assessments_of_Stepinac.27s_actions_during_WWII"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Overall_assessments_of_Stepinac's_actions_during_WWII">Overall assessments of Stepinac's actions during WWII</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Overall assessments of Stepinac&#039;s actions during WWII">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"/><table class="box-POV plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-POV" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><img alt="Unbalanced scales.svg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Unbalanced_scales.svg/45px-Unbalanced_scales.svg.png" decoding="async" width="45" height="40" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Unbalanced_scales.svg/68px-Unbalanced_scales.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Unbalanced_scales.svg/90px-Unbalanced_scales.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="354" /></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">The <b><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Neutral_point_of_view" title="Wikipedia:Neutral point of view">neutrality</a> of this section is <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NPOV_dispute" title="Wikipedia:NPOV dispute">disputed</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Relevant discussion may be found on the <a href="/wiki/Talk:Aloysius_Stepinac##" title="Talk:Aloysius Stepinac">talk page</a>. Please do not remove this message until <a href="/wiki/Template:POV#When_to_remove" title="Template:POV">conditions to do so are met</a>.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">August 2018</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"/><table class="box-Essay-like plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-style ambox-essay-like" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/f/f2/Edit-clear.svg/40px-Edit-clear.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="40" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/f/f2/Edit-clear.svg/60px-Edit-clear.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/f/f2/Edit-clear.svg/80px-Edit-clear.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="48" data-file-height="48" /></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>is written like a <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:What_Wikipedia_is_not#Wikipedia_is_not_a_publisher_of_original_thought" title="Wikipedia:What Wikipedia is not">personal reflection, personal essay, or argumentative essay</a></b> that states a Wikipedia editor's personal feelings or presents an original argument about a topic.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit">help improve it</a> by rewriting it in an <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Writing_better_articles#Information_style_and_tone" title="Wikipedia:Writing better articles">encyclopedic style</a>.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">August 2018</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Tomasevich assesses Stepinac's wartime words and deeds in three categories: (1) Private actions to help certain individuals and groups, where he states Stepinac "deserves highest praise, although his actions were not always successful",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001563_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001563-175">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> (2) Proclamations of general human rights, expressed in his sermons at first "occasionally and mildly", but more strongly after 1943 (after the Ustashe had already perpetrated most of the genocides, and it was clear the Nazis and Ustashe would be defeated), also deserve praise,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001563_175-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001563-175">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> and (3) Statements dealing with overall Church policy toward the wartime Croatian state. Here Tomasevich states "there are serious shortcomings in Stepinac's statements and actions toward the Ustashe regime and its genocidal actions against the Serbs and the Serbian Orthodox Church".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001564_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001564-176">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thus, despite the Ustashe genocide against the Serbs, the ethnic cleansing and forced conversions, the killings and expulsions of most Orthodox priests, the destruction and desecration of many Orthodox Churches, "neither Archbishop Stepinac, nor any other Catholic bishop in the state&#160;... uttered one word of public protest" against these crimes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001537_119-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001537-119">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> "This was not a mere oversight. It was a deliberate policy" as stated by the papal legate Abbot Marcone, when he wrote in 1943 that given the fact that most guerrillas were "schismatics" (i.e. Eastern-Orthodox Serbs) "our Croatian episcopate does not have any special motive to protest publicly against the government in favor of the schismatics".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001538_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001538-177">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> In Stepinac's case this was compounded by his "dim view of Orthodoxy and thought that an unbridgeable gulf existed between Croats and Serbs".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001564_176-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001564-176">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, given both Stepinac's and the Vatican's desire to see a Catholic state in Croatia and have a legate there, "the Church had to refrain from publicly criticizing the government's policies".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001564_176-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001564-176">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tomasevich concludes "the policy of the Croatian Catholic Church hierarchy and the Vatican of not publicly condemning the Ustasha regime's actions during the war will probably remain controversial. From the standpoint of humanity, justice and common decency, it cannot be defended. But Stepinac was not the only one responsible for it, the Vatican was too".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001564_176-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001564-176">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stella Alexander, author of <i>The Triple Myth</i>, a sympathetic biography of Stepinac, writes about him that
</p>
<blockquote><p>Two things stand out. He feared Communism above all (especially above fascism); and he found it hard to grasp that anything beyond the boundaries of Croatia, always excepting the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy See</a>, was quite real.&#160;... He lived in the midst of apocalyptic events, bearing responsibilities which he had not sought.&#160;... In the end one is left feeling that he was not quite great enough for his role. Given his limitations he behaved very well, certainly much better than most of his own people, and he grew in spiritual stature during the course of his long ordeal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKaplan201417,_19–20_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaplan201417,_19–20-178">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In another article, Stella Alexander writes the following:
</p>
<blockquote><p>From May 1942 he attacked the actions of the [ustaše] government in sermon after sermon, not only the forcible conversions but the anti-semitism and anti-Serbianism of the regime, the taking and shooting of hostages and the forcible breaking-up of Jewish-Gentile marriages, and he wrote bitterly to Pavelić about the conditions in the concentration camps, particularly the one at Jasenovac. He made repeated private interventions in individual cases, he refused to allow converted Jews to wear the yellow star in church, and he forbad military chaplains to administer the ustaSa oath if a crossed dagger and revolver were lying in front of the crucifix. Eventually he arranged for about 7,000 children, who were either orphans or had lost their families, to be accepted into Catholic homes, but forbad the clergy to baptize them into the Catholic Church. The ustaša authorities were furious with him, Pavelić detested him and according to Fr Masucci, secretary of the Vatican representative in Zagreb, asked the Vatican on three occasions to withdraw him. His friend Ivan Meštrović the sculptor, who met him in Rome during one of Stepinac's visits to the Vatican, wrote later in his memoirs that Stepinac told him that he expected to be killed either by the ustaSe or the communists.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander1978_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander1978-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p><a href="/wiki/Mark_Biondich" title="Mark Biondich">Mark Biondich</a> stated that Stepinac was not an "ardent supporter" of the Ustaše regime legitimating their every policy, but neither was he an "avowed opponent" publicly denouncing their crimes in a systematic manner, that many of his defenders claim.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2006_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2006-179">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-war_period">Post-war period</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Post-war period">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Socialist_Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia" title="Socialist Federal Republic of Yugoslavia">Socialist Federal Republic of Yugoslavia</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Event_in_Zagreb_in_honour_of_Tito.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Event_in_Zagreb_in_honour_of_Tito.jpg/220px-Event_in_Zagreb_in_honour_of_Tito.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Event_in_Zagreb_in_honour_of_Tito.jpg/330px-Event_in_Zagreb_in_honour_of_Tito.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Event_in_Zagreb_in_honour_of_Tito.jpg/440px-Event_in_Zagreb_in_honour_of_Tito.jpg 2x" data-file-width="576" data-file-height="431" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Event_in_Zagreb_in_honour_of_Tito.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Stepinac at a post-war communist rally in September 1945. From left: three dignitaries of the Orthodox Church, the Partisan General Commanding of Zagreb, the Secretary to the Apostolic Visitor, Auxiliary Bishop Dr. <a href="/w/index.php?title=Josip_Lach&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Josip Lach (page does not exist)">Josip Lach</a>, Archbishop Stepinac, People's Premier of Croatia Dr. <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Bakaric" class="mw-redirect" title="Vladimir Bakaric">Vladimir Bakaric</a>, Soviet Military Attache, Minister of the Interior Dr. Hebrang.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Brien194737–38_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Brien194737–38-180">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Marija_Bistrica_bazilika.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Marija_Bistrica_bazilika.jpg/220px-Marija_Bistrica_bazilika.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Marija_Bistrica_bazilika.jpg/330px-Marija_Bistrica_bazilika.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Marija_Bistrica_bazilika.jpg/440px-Marija_Bistrica_bazilika.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Marija_Bistrica_bazilika.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Our Lady of <a href="/wiki/Marija_Bistrica" title="Marija Bistrica">Marija Bistrica</a>, where <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatified</a> Stepinac before 500,000 Croatians</div></div></div>
<p>The Allies recognized the new Tito–Šubašić Yugoslav government in 1944. In February 1945 the Zagreb Archdiocese newspaper reported that Stepinac and other Church dignitaries attended a congratulatory New Year's reception at <a href="/wiki/Ante_Paveli%C4%87" title="Ante Pavelić">Pavelić</a>’s, which included members of the Allied German Armed Forces, led by <i><a href="/wiki/Generalfeldmarschall" title="Generalfeldmarschall">Generalfeldmarschall</a></i> <a href="/wiki/Maximilian_von_Weichs" title="Maximilian von Weichs">Maximilian von Weichs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> On March 24, 1945, as the Partisans fought their final pitched battles against retreating German, NDH and other collaborationist forces, Stepinac and the Croatian bishops, at the behest of the Ustaše regime, issued a pastoral letter asking Croats to remain loyal to the NDH,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001550_136-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001550-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> and stand firm against the advancing Partisans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880_109-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> On April 10, 1945, Stepinac celebrated his last Te Deum for the NDH, which still had Race Laws, and was just then killing the last remaining 3,500 inmates, including 700-800 Jews, at the Jasenovac concentration camp.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018605-621_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018605-621-182">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> In the final days of the war Stepinac received and hid the archives of the Ustaše Ministry of Foreign Affairs, which later proved to contain gold stolen from concentration camp inmates.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880_109-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Shortly after the fall of the NDH and during the <a href="/wiki/Surrender_at_Bleiburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Surrender at Bleiburg">surrender at Bleiburg</a>, on 17 May 1945, Stepinac was effectively put under house arrest in Zagreb and was for the subsequent two weeks interviewed by <a href="/wiki/Socialist_Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia" title="Socialist Federal Republic of Yugoslavia">Yugoslavia</a>′s <a href="/wiki/OZNA" title="OZNA">security</a>, military judicial, and <a href="/wiki/League_of_Communists_of_Yugoslavia" title="League of Communists of Yugoslavia">Communist party</a> officials such as Veljko Drakulić, Vladimir Ranogajec, Antun Biber Tehek, whose aim was apparently to sound out his political stances with a view to finding a mode of political co-existence under the new regime.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 2 June, Yugoslav leader <a href="/wiki/Josip_Broz_Tito" title="Josip Broz Tito">Josip Broz Tito</a> met with representatives of the Archdiocese of Zagreb,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAkmadža2003_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkmadža2003-184">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> during which he advocated the idea that "the Catholic Church could do more for the people if it was independent of the Vatican" and more "national", like the Serbian Orthodox Church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKolar2008249_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKolar2008249-185">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> The following day, Archbishop Stepinac was released from custody. One day later, Stepinac met with Tito, during which Tito's prime goal was to promote the idea of an autonomous Catholic Church for Yugoslavia with its own <a href="/wiki/Primate_(bishop)" title="Primate (bishop)">primate</a>. This was consistent with the policy of the Yugoslav government in the immediate post-war period.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKolar2008244_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKolar2008244-186">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> The meeting with Tito, <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Bakari%C4%87" title="Vladimir Bakarić">Vladimir Bakarić</a> in attendance, on 4 June 1945 was written up in detail by Stepinac, both as a personal memo and as a report to <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 22 June, the bishops of <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Republic_of_Croatia" class="mw-redirect" title="People&#39;s Republic of Croatia">Croatia</a> released a public letter accusing the Yugoslav authorities of injustices and crimes towards them. On 28 June, Stepinac wrote a letter to the government of Croatia asking for an end to the prosecution of Nazi collaborationists<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamet200285_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamet200285-188">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> (collaboration having been widespread in occupied Yugoslavia) and prosecutions were being used by some as a pretext to settle old scores. On 10 July, Stepinac's secretary, Stjepan Lacković, traveled to Rome. While he was there, the Yugoslav authorities forbade him to return.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> Lacković went instead to the United States.
</p><p>In August, a new land reform law was introduced which legalized the confiscation of 85 percent of church holdings in Yugoslavia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELilly200147_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELilly200147-190">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> During the same period the archbishop almost certainly had ties with the post-war <a href="/wiki/Usta%C5%A1e" title="Ustaše">Ustaše</a>  guerrillas, the "<a href="/wiki/Crusaders_(Usta%C5%A1e)" class="mw-redirect" title="Crusaders (Ustaše)">Crusaders</a>",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285_1-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> and actively worked against the state.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander1987_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander1987-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2015)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> After fleeing with Pavelić, Erih Lisak, the last Ustaše chief of police, secretly returned to Croatia in September 1945, to organize remaining Ustaše forces hiding in forests, and he established contact with Stepinac's office and Stepinac himself.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001560_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001560-191">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> Ante Moškov, a former Ustaše general, also contacted Stepinac, and the police discovered Ustaše files and gold hidden in the archbishopric cellars.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001560_191-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001560-191">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac urged Tito to meet with representatives of the Croatian Peasant party and even the Ustaše to help heal the wounds of war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamet1990185_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamet1990185-192">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 1945, a synod of the <a href="/wiki/Bishops%27_Conference_of_Yugoslavia" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishops&#39; Conference of Yugoslavia">Bishops' Conference of Yugoslavia</a> was held in Zagreb which discussed the confrontation with the government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001559_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001559-193">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> On 20. September, Stepinac published a pastoral letter in which he stated that "273 clergymen had been killed" since the Partisan take-over, "169 had been imprisoned", and another "89 were missing and presumed dead".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001572_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001572-194">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> The bishops also criticized the virtual suppression of the Catholic press, the fact that religious education was restricted, the confiscation of the majority of church lands, and the confiscation of seminaries. They condemned all ideologies based on a materialist atheistic philosophy. They strongly condemned the introduction of civil marriages, in addition to church marriages.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197878_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197878-195">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Goldstein notes that Stepinac publicly, by name criticized the Communists even before they took power, with the pastoral letter of March 1945 read from all the pulpits across the NDH, again repeated with the pastoral letter of September 1945.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582-583_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582-583-196">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> This is very different from Stepinac's criticisms of the Ustaše in front of limited audiences, never issuing a pastoral letter against the Ustaše, never even criticizing them by name, and only after the Ustaše had completed most of their genocides.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582-583_196-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582-583-196">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> Simultaneously, Stepinac continued to publicly support the same NDH which committed those crimes, until the very end.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582-583_196-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein2018582-583-196">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> The pastoral letter's extensive criticism of Communist actions against the Catholic Church, also contrasted with Stepinac's complete silence, noted by Tomasevich, regarding the massive Ustaše attacks on the Serbian Orthodox Church - e.g. the Ustaše killing and exiling of 85% of Orthodox priests, efforts to eliminate the Serbian church and replace it with an Ustaše-created Orthodox church, etc.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531_172-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In response to this letter Tito spoke out publicly against Stepinac for the first time by writing an editorial on 25 October in the communist party's newspaper <i>Borba</i> accusing Stepinac of declaring war on the fledgling new Yugoslavia. Consequently, on 4 November  a crowd of Partisans threw stones at Stepinac in <a href="/wiki/Zapre%C5%A1i%C4%87" title="Zaprešić">Zaprešić</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAkmadža200424_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkmadža200424-197">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETanner1997180_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanner1997180-198">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> Tito had established "<a href="/wiki/Brotherhood_and_unity" title="Brotherhood and unity">brotherhood and unity</a>" as the federation's overarching objective and central policy, one which he did not want threatened by internal agitation. In addition, with the escalating Cold War conflict and increased concerns over both Western and Soviet infiltration (see <a href="/wiki/Tito%E2%80%93Stalin_split" title="Tito–Stalin split">Tito–Stalin split</a>), the Yugoslav government did not tolerate further internal subversion within the potentially fragile new federation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285_1-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In an effort to put a stop to the archbishop's activities, Tito attempted to reach an accord with Stepinac, and achieve a greater degree of independence for the Catholic Church in Yugoslavia and Croatia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander1979_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander1979-199">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2015)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Stepinac refused to break from the Vatican, and continued to publicly condemn the communist government. Tito, however, was reluctant to bring him to trial, in spite of condemning evidence which was available.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285_1-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> In January 1946 the federal government attempted to solicit his replacement with the Papal Nuncio in Belgrade, a request that was denied. Finally, Stepinac was himself asked to leave the country, which he refused.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2011)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In September 1946 the Yugoslav authorities indicted Stepinac on multiple counts of <a href="/wiki/War_crimes" class="mw-redirect" title="War crimes">war crimes</a> and <a href="/wiki/Collaborationism" class="mw-redirect" title="Collaborationism">collaboration</a> with the enemy during wartime.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285_1-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Milovan_%C4%90ilas" class="mw-redirect" title="Milovan Đilas">Milovan Đilas</a>, a prominent leader in the Party, stated that Stepinac would never have been brought to trial "had he not continued to oppose the new Communist regime."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETanner1997180_198-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanner1997180-198">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> Stella Alexander suggests that Yugoslav authorities wished to balance the arrest and execution of Serb leader <a href="/wiki/Dra%C5%BEa_Mihailovi%C4%87" title="Draža Mihailović">Draža Mihailović</a> by moving against a prominent Croat; and in the words of the Public Prosecutor, "to unmask before the world a concerted conspiracy by the western imperial powers against the new Yugoslavia".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197879_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197879-200">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stepinac publicly condemned the new Yugoslav government and its actions during and after World War II, especially for murders of priests by Communist militants.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson199990–92_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunsonBunsonBunson199990–92-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Trial">Trial</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Trial">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Alojzije_Stepinac_on_trial.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Alojzije_Stepinac_on_trial.jpg/220px-Alojzije_Stepinac_on_trial.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Alojzije_Stepinac_on_trial.jpg/330px-Alojzije_Stepinac_on_trial.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/3e/Alojzije_Stepinac_on_trial.jpg 2x" data-file-width="350" data-file-height="233" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Alojzije_Stepinac_on_trial.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Archbishop Stepinac on trial</div></div></div>
<p>By September of the same year the Yugoslav authorities indicted Stepinac on several counts—<a href="/wiki/Collaboration" title="Collaboration">collaboration</a> with the occupation forces, relations with the Ustaše regime, having chaplains in the Ustaše army as religious agitators, forced conversions of Serb Orthodox to Catholicism at gunpoint and high treason against the Yugoslav government. Stepinac was arrested on 18 September 1946 and his trial started on 30 September 1946, where he was tried alongside former officials of the Ustaše government including Erih Lisak (sentenced to death) and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Ivan_%C5%A0ali%C4%87&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Ivan Šalić (page does not exist)">Ivan Šalić</a>. Altogether there were 19 defendants.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197879_200-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197879-200">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The prosecution presented their evidence for the archbishop's collaboration with the Ustaše regime.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285_1-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine2007284–285-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander1987_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander1987-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2015)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In relation to these events the prosecution pointed out that even if the archbishop did not explicitly order them, he also did nothing to stop them or punish those within the church who were responsible. The Court appointed two distinguished advocates to defend Stepinac – Dr. Politeo and Dr. Katicic.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197884_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197884-201">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac defense acknowledged he paid courtesy calls on Pavelić and other Ustaše leaders as soon as they arrived, but this could be considered normal for the head of the Catholic Church. However, Alexander notes that Stepinac's tone of welcome to Ustaše authorities could have left the clergy in no doubt that they were expected to cooperate with them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880_109-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Alexander claims his lawyer defended him more convincingly on some other accusations, stating that Stepinac's control over the censored Catholic press had been largely formal, and that he had no control outside his own diocese.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880_109-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> When he was appointed NDH military vicar, Stepinac's two deputies, both prominent Ustaše supporters, had already been appointed and it would’ve been virtually impossible for him to remove them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880_109-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> He had accepted Ustaše archives without examining them, and stowed them in the archbishopric, but later handed them over to the Partisans when they came looking for them. He admitted meeting with former Ustaše chief of police, Erih Lisak, who had secretly returned to Zagreb to fight the new authorities, but Stepinac blamed his secretary for the meeting.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880_109-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197880-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Alexander states that Stepinac seemed most uncertain when defending himself against the most serious accusations of forced conversions of the Serbian Orthodox population to Catholicism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander197881_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander197881-58">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 3 October as part of the fourth day of the proceedings, Stepinac gave a lengthy 38-minute speech during which he laid down his views on the legitimacy of the trial. He claimed that the process was a "show trial", that he was being attacked in order for the state to attack the Church, and that "no religious conversions were done in bad faith".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001-80">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2015)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> He went on to state that "My conscience is clear and calm. If you will not give me the right, history will give me that right", and that he did not intend to defend himself or appeal against a conviction, and that he is prepared to take ridicule, disdain, humiliation, and death for his beliefs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPatte1953_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatte1953-202">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2015)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> He claimed that the military vicariate in the Independent State of Croatia was created to address the needs of the faithful among the soldiers and not for the army itself, nor as a sign of approval of all action by the army. He stated that he was never an Ustaša and that his Croatian nationalism stemmed from the nation's grievances in the Serb-dominated Kingdom of Yugoslavia, and that he never took part in any anti-government or terrorist activities against the state or against Serbs.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stepinac also pointed to the fact that Yugoslav communists killed a great number of Catholic priests - on 20 September 1945 he already published a letter to the faithful where he brought to light the fact that at least 243 Catholic priests were summarily executed by the communist <a href="/wiki/Yugoslav_Partisans" title="Yugoslav Partisans">Yugoslav army</a> for (alleged or real) collaboration with the <a href="/wiki/Independent_State_of_Croatia" title="Independent State of Croatia">NDH</a>, with other 169 imprisoned and 89 missing priests.<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> Tomasevich notes that all sides killed priests, starting first with the Ustaše who killed 157 Orthodox priests, including 3 Orthodox bishops. They jailed, tortured and expelled to Serbia the <a href="/wiki/Dositej_Vasi%C4%87" title="Dositej Vasić">Orthodox archbishop of Zagreb</a>, along with 327 more Orthodox priests.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001528_170-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001528-170">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> Yet Tomasevich writes that neither Stepinac nor anyone else in the Catholic Church hierarchy uttered a single word of protest against these killings and other massive attacks on the Orthodox church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001555_91-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001555-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> On the contrary, Tomasevich states that the massive Ustaše attack on the Serbian Orthodox Church "was approved and supported by many Croatian Catholic priests"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531_172-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001531-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> He also writes that in the NDH some Catholic priests joined the Ustaše, serving in the Ustaše government apparatus, others denounced Partisans, causing their death or deportation to concentration camps, a few became Ustaše officers serving in concentration camps.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001568–569_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001568–569-205">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> Above all, Catholic priests engaged in propaganda work for the Ustaše state. Tomasevich notes that most priests killed in the war's aftermath were not killed because they belonged to a specific religion, but because they were participants in a civil war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001568–569_205-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001568–569-205">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stepinac was arrested on 18 September, and was only given the indictment on the 23rd−meaning his defense were given only six to seven days to prepare.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Brien194780–89_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Brien194780–89-206">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac's defense counsel was only allowed to call twenty witnesses—while the prosecution was allowed to call fifty-eight, most from outside Stepinac's diocese. The President of the Court refused to hear fourteen witnesses for the defense.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamet1990189_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamet1990189-207">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 11 October 1946, the court found Stepinac guilty of collaboration with the fascist Ustaše regime.<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> He was sentenced to 16 years in prison. He served five years in the prison at Lepoglava until he was released in a conciliatory gesture by Tito, on condition that he either retire to Rome or be confined to his home parish of Krašić. He chose to stay in Krašić, saying he would never leave "unless they put me on a plane by force and take me over the frontier."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETanner1997186_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanner1997186-209">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Biondich, Stepinac's conviction for high treason was political, given that the Yugoslav authorities had a vested interest in it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a44_137-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich2007a44-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> Professor Bogdan Kolar of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Ljubljana" title="University of Ljubljana">University of Ljubljana</a> notes that the chief trial prosecutor, <a href="/wiki/Jakov_Bla%C5%BEevi%C4%87" title="Jakov Blažević">Jakov Blažević</a>, admitted in a 1985 interview with the Slovenian magazine <i><a href="/wiki/Delo_(newspaper)" title="Delo (newspaper)">Polet</a></i> that "Stepinac's only crime was not partaking in the separation of the Church in Croatia from the Vatican."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKolar2008250_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKolar2008250-210">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tomasevich writes that the government's failure to provide the media and historians full access to all pre-trial and trial materials, puts Stepinac's guilt in question.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001563_175-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001563-175">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> At the same time Tomasevich notes that Stepinac's failure to publicly condemn the Ustaše's genocidal measures against Serbs and the Orthodox Church, "cannot be defended from the standpoint of humanity, justice and common decency".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001564_176-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasevich2001564-176">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> On the other hand, John Fine states that the trial "was well publicized both at home and abroad by extremely biased figures on both sides, was carried out with the proper legal procedures; there was no torture and a great deal of evidence was brought before the judges, a considerable amount of which was devastating and accurate, and clearly demonstrated the archbishop's collaboration with the Ustaše regime."<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Yugoslav Communist trial against Stepinac's is seen by some as a part of coordinated effort by the Eastern Europe communist regimes to severe Catholic Church in their countries from Rome, and was near contemporaneous with the Communist trials against the Cardinals <a href="/wiki/Josyf_Slipyj" title="Josyf Slipyj">Josyf Slipyj</a> of Ukraine, <a href="/wiki/Jozsef_Mindszenty" class="mw-redirect" title="Jozsef Mindszenty">Jozsef Mindszenty</a> of Hungary, <a href="/wiki/Josef_Beran" title="Josef Beran">Josef Beran</a> of Czechoslovakia, and <a href="/wiki/Stefan_Wyszynski" class="mw-redirect" title="Stefan Wyszynski">Stefan Wyszynski</a> of Poland.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Reactions">Reactions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Reactions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the escalating <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> atmosphere, and with the Vatican putting forward worldwide publicity,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander1987_5-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander1987-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2015)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> the trial was depicted in the West as a typical communist "show trial", in which the testimony was all false. The trial was immediately condemned by the Holy See. All Catholics who had taken part in the court proceedings, including most of the jury members, were excommunicated by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> who referred to the process as the "saddest trial" (<i>tristissimo processo</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVelikonja2003198_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVelikonja2003198-213">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the United States, one of Stepinac's biggest supporters was the Archbishop of Boston, <a href="/wiki/Richard_Cushing" title="Richard Cushing">Richard Cushing</a>, who delivered several sermons in support of him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDever1975135_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDever1975135-214">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> U.S. Acting Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Dean_Acheson" title="Dean Acheson">Dean Acheson</a> on 11 October 1946 bemoaned the conditions in Yugoslavia and stated his regret of the trial.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Brien194780–89_206-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Brien194780–89-206">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/National_Conference_of_Christians_and_Jews" class="mw-redirect" title="National Conference of Christians and Jews">National Conference of Christians and Jews</a> at the Bronx Round Table<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2018)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> adopted a unanimous resolution on 13 October condemning the trial:
</p>
<blockquote><p>This great churchman has been charged with being a collaborator with the Nazis. We Jews deny that. We know from his record since 1934, that he was a true friend of the Jews&#160;... This man, now the victim of a sham trial, all during the Nazi regime spoke out openly, unafraid, against the dreadful Nuremberg Laws, and his opposition to the Nazi terrorism was never relaxed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Brien194780–89_206-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Brien194780–89-206">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">Britain</a>, on 23 October 1946, Richard Stokes MP declared in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> that,
</p>
<blockquote><p>[T]he archbishop was our constant ally in 1941, during the worst of the crisis, and thereafter, at a time when the Orthodox Church, which is now <i>comme il faut</i> with the Tito Government, was shaking hands with Mussolini.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>On 1 November 1946 <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> addressed the House of Commons on the subject of the trial, expressing "great sadness" at the result.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAkmadža200458_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkmadža200458-216">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>This trial was prepared in the political sphere. It was for the purpose of dividing the Catholic Church in Croatia from its leadership at the Vatican. Tito has openly expressed this purpose.&#160;... The trial was not based on justice, but was an outrage on justice. Tito's regime has no interest in justice. It seeks only to stifle opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Brien194780–89_206-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;Brien194780–89-206">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Annulment_of_the_verdict">Annulment of the verdict</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Annulment of the verdict">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On 22 July 2016, Zagreb County Court annulled the verdict in the review process, as requested by the Archbishop's nephew Boris Stepinac, "due to gross violations of current and former fundamental principles of substantive and procedural criminal law". While explaining the verdict, Judge Ivan Turudić stated that the verdict had violated the principle of the <a href="/wiki/Right_to_a_fair_trial" title="Right to a fair trial">right to a fair trial</a>, appeal and a reasoned court decision, as well as the principle of the prohibition of forced labour and the rule of law, adding that it was enough to read some parts of the minutes of the trial, which demonstrated that the court mainly analyzed evidence that instructed witnesses for the prosecution to charge the defendant and that the actual decision in reality was not made by the court but by prosecutor Jakov Blažević. In conclusion, Turudić stated that the verdict has been revised after nearly 70 years which has a profound significance for the history of the Croatian people.<sup id="cite_ref-index1_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-index1-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> This annulment is, however, highly controversial due to its overtly nationalist Croatian sentiments, the fact that lower court (County Court of Zagreb) annuls the verdict of the Supreme Court, the fact that the whole process of annulment was over in only six days,<sup id="cite_ref-Veritas_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Veritas-217">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> prosecution was on the same side with the defense (they did not appeal on the annulment) and the judge, who is openly anticommunist,<sup id="cite_ref-Večernji1_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Večernji1-218">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> obviously made his decision before the start of the trial.<sup id="cite_ref-Večernji2_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Večernji2-219">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> Thus, this annulment can be seen as an example of a <a href="/wiki/Show_trial" title="Show trial">show trial</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Novosti_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Novosti-220">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Simon Wiesenthal Center strongly condemned the annulment, stating Stepinac “openly supported the regime which committed mass murder, and afforded them spiritual comfort and support... Right now in Croatia there is a cultural, ideological war,” with a segment seeking to whitewash or modify the crimes of the Ustasha.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-221">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> The head of the Wiesenthal Center, Efraim Zuroff, also stated that annulment in Croatia was part of a much wider phenomenon, which he has also observed in Ukraine, Lithuania and Hungary – “the tendency to honor people who fought communism, without checking what they did in WWII.” Following the annulment, the Yad Vashem Center reaffirmed that the reason for the Committee for the Designation of the Righteous Among the Nations did not grant Stepinac Righteous status “was due to the archbishop's close ties to the Ustasha regime.”<sup id="cite_ref-:4_221-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-221">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Imprisonment">Imprisonment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Imprisonment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Alojzije_Stepinac_Rozga.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Alojzije_Stepinac_Rozga.jpg/220px-Alojzije_Stepinac_Rozga.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="281" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Alojzije_Stepinac_Rozga.jpg/330px-Alojzije_Stepinac_Rozga.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Alojzije_Stepinac_Rozga.jpg/440px-Alojzije_Stepinac_Rozga.jpg 2x" data-file-width="904" data-file-height="1154" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Alojzije_Stepinac_Rozga.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Bust of Stepinac at the village of Rozga near Zagreb.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Sarcophagus_of_blessed_Alojzije_Stepinac_in_Zagreb.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Sarcophagus_of_blessed_Alojzije_Stepinac_in_Zagreb.jpg/220px-Sarcophagus_of_blessed_Alojzije_Stepinac_in_Zagreb.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Sarcophagus_of_blessed_Alojzije_Stepinac_in_Zagreb.jpg/330px-Sarcophagus_of_blessed_Alojzije_Stepinac_in_Zagreb.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Sarcophagus_of_blessed_Alojzije_Stepinac_in_Zagreb.jpg/440px-Sarcophagus_of_blessed_Alojzije_Stepinac_in_Zagreb.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2516" data-file-height="1887" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Sarcophagus_of_blessed_Alojzije_Stepinac_in_Zagreb.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Stepinac's grave in the <a href="/wiki/Zagreb_Cathedral" title="Zagreb Cathedral">Zagreb Cathedral</a></div></div></div>
<p>In Stepinac's absence, the Archbishop of <a href="/wiki/Belgrade" title="Belgrade">Belgrade</a> Josip Ujčić became acting president of the <a href="/wiki/Bishops%27_Conference_of_Yugoslavia" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishops&#39; Conference of Yugoslavia">Bishops' Conference of Yugoslavia</a>, a position he held until Stepinac's death.<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> In March 1947 the president of the government of the <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Republic_of_Croatia" class="mw-redirect" title="People&#39;s Republic of Croatia">People's Republic of Croatia</a> <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Bakari%C4%87" title="Vladimir Bakarić">Vladimir Bakarić</a> made an official visit to <a href="/wiki/Lepoglava_prison" title="Lepoglava prison">Lepoglava prison</a> to see Stepinac.<sup id="cite_ref-Jandric_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jandric-223">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He offered that he sign a plea for amnesty to Yugoslavia's leader <a href="/wiki/Josip_Broz_Tito" title="Josip Broz Tito">Josip Broz</a>, who would, in turn, allow Stepinac to leave the country. Instead, Stepinac gave Bakarić a request to Broz that he be retried by a neutral court.<sup id="cite_ref-Jandric_223-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jandric-223">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> He also offered to explain his actions to the Croatian people on the <a href="/wiki/Ban_Jela%C4%8Di%C4%87_Square" title="Ban Jelačić Square">largest square in Zagreb</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Jandric_223-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jandric-223">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> A positive response was not received from either request. During his imprisonment, Stepinac condemned the "clerical societies" being encouraged by the government as a way of developing more "nationally-aligned" churches.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKolar2008250_210-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKolar2008250-210">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The 1947 pilgrimage to Marija Bistrica attracted 75,000 people.<sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> Dragutin Saili had been in charge of the pilgrimage on the part of the Yugoslav authorities. At a meeting of the Central Committee on 1 August 1947 Saili was chastised for allowing pictures of Stepinac to be carried during the pilgrimage, as long as the pictures were alongside those of Yugoslav leader Josip Broz.<sup id="cite_ref-Zapisnici_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zapisnici-225">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> Marko Belinić responded to the report by saying, "<i>Saili's path, his poor cooperation with the Local Committee, is a deadly thing</i>".<sup id="cite_ref-Zapisnici_225-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Zapisnici-225">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 1949, the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">United States House of Representatives</a> approved a resolution condemning Stepinac's imprisonment, with the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">Senate</a> following suit several months later.<sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> Aloysius Stepinac eventually served five years of his sixteen-year sentence for <a href="/wiki/High_treason" class="mw-redirect" title="High treason">high treason</a> in the Lepoglava prison, where he received preferred treatment in recognition of his clerical status. He was allocated two cells for personal use and an additional cell as his private chapel, while being exempt of all hard labor.<sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1950 the noted Irish essayist and historian, <a href="/wiki/Hubert_Butler" title="Hubert Butler">Hubert Butler</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Quakers" title="Quakers">Quaker</a> friends managed to visit Stepinac in prison. Troubled by the Catholic Church's support of the <a href="/wiki/Usta%C5%A1e" title="Ustaše">Ustaše</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup> and the Church's role in the wartime conversions of Orthodox to Catholicism,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAgee2002156_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAgee2002156-229">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> Butler asked Stepinac why he had appointed as his deputy on conversions, the bishop Josip Šimrak, an enthusiastic advocate of converting the Orthodox. To which Butler noted, “the archbishop gave the stock reply he had so often given at the trial – <i>notre conscience est tranquille (our conscience is clear).”<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAgee2002157_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAgee2002157-230">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup></i>
</p><p>In 1950, a group of United States senators made foreign aid to Yugoslavia conditional on Stepinac's release.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGitman201196_231-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitman201196-231">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> On 11 November 1951, <a href="/wiki/C._L._Sulzberger" title="C. L. Sulzberger">Cyrus L. Sulzberger</a> from <i><a href="/wiki/The_New_York_Times" title="The New York Times">The New York Times</a></i> visited Stepinac in Lepoglava.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAkmadža200462_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkmadža200462-232">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> He won the <a href="/wiki/1951_Pulitzer_Prize" title="1951 Pulitzer Prize">Pulitzer Prize</a> for the interview.<sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A visiting congressional delegation from the United States, including <a href="/wiki/Clement_J._Zablocki" title="Clement J. Zablocki">Clement J. Zablocki</a> and <a href="/wiki/Edna_F._Kelly" title="Edna F. Kelly">Edna F. Kelly</a>, pressed to see Stepinac in late November 1951. Their request was denied by the Yugoslav authorities, but Josip Broz Tito assured the delegation that Stepinac would be released within a month.<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac was released as a precondition for American aid, on the condition that he either retire to Rome or be confined to his home parish of Krašić. He refused to leave Yugoslavia and opted to live in Krašić under a form of <a href="/wiki/House_arrest" title="House arrest">house arrest</a>, to which he was transferred on 5 December 1951. Among the reporters awaiting Stepinac in his native Krasic were those from the American Time Magazine, who reported that Stepinac "walked easily and firmly", stating of his prison days that “he was not maltreated, could say Mass, have visitors, books and newspapers”. When asked about the conditions of his release, Stepinac responded: "I was not released under any conditions. They released me on their own wish...The reason I did not ask to be released is that I don't feel guilty."<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> He lived in the parish <a href="/wiki/Clergy_house" title="Clergy house">presbytery</a> and was able to say Mass in the adjacent church. He stated that: "They will never make me leave unless they put me on a plane by force and take me over the frontier. It is my duty in these difficult times to stay with the people."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETanner1997186_209-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanner1997186-209">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At a meeting of the Central Committee of the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Communists_of_Croatia" title="League of Communists of Croatia">Communist Party of Croatia</a> on 5 October 1951, Ivan Krajačić said, "In America they are printing <i>Crvena ruža na oltaru</i> [<i>Red Roses on the Altar</i>] of 350 pages, in which is described the entire Stepinac process. Religious education is particularly recently being taught on a large scale. We should do something about this. We could ban religious education. We could ban religious education in schools, but they will then pass it into their churches".<sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> On 31 January 1952 the Yugoslav authorities abolished religious education in state-run public schools, as part of the programme of separating church and state in Yugoslavia. In April, Stepinac told a journalist from Belgium's <i>La Libertea</i>, "<i>I am greatly concerned about Catholic youth. In schools they are carrying out intensive communist propaganda, based on negating the truth</i>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAkmadža200493–95_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkmadža200493–95-237">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cardinalate">Cardinalate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Cardinalate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>On 29 November 1952, his name appeared in a list of cardinals to be newly created by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a>; the day coincided with Yugoslavia's Republic Day.<sup id="cite_ref-Luxmore_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Luxmore-238">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> (Stepinac was created a cardinal in 1953, but would never go to Rome to receive the red hat and titular church.) Yugoslavia then severed diplomatic relations with the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Vatican</a> in October 1953.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest1995286_239-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest1995286-239">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup> In 1954, Stepinac received a rare visit from a Swedish journalist, to whom he said, "I tried to save, and did save, thousands of lives", and "[a]s for the massacres in the churches, what could I do?"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETanner1997186_209-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanner1997186-209">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> The government also expelled the Catholic Faculty of Theology from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Zagreb" title="University of Zagreb">University of Zagreb</a>, to which it was not restored until the first democratic elections were held in 1990, and was finally formalized in 1996.<sup id="cite_ref-240" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-240">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-241" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-241">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-242" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-242">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pius XII wrote to Cardinal Stepinac and three other jailed prelates (Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Stefan_Wyszy%C5%84ski" title="Stefan Wyszyński">Stefan Wyszyński</a>, Cardinal <a href="/wiki/J%C3%B3zsef_Mindszenty" title="József Mindszenty">József Mindszenty</a>, and future Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Josef_Beran" title="Josef Beran">Josef Beran</a>) on 29 June 1956 urging their supporters to remain loyal.<sup id="cite_ref-Luxmore_238-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Luxmore-238">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> Stepinac was unable to participate in the <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_1958" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, 1958">1958 Papal conclave</a> due to his house arrest, despite calls from the <a href="/wiki/Bishops%27_Conference_of_Yugoslavia" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishops&#39; Conference of Yugoslavia">Bishops' Conference of Yugoslavia</a> for his release.<sup id="cite_ref-243" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-243">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-244" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-244">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> On 2 June 1959 he wrote in a letter to Ivan Meštrović: "I likely will not live to see the collapse of communism in the world due to my poor health. But I am absolutely certain of that collapse."<sup id="cite_ref-Tomic_245-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tomic-245">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to a 2020 report per the Croatian ambassador to the Holy See, Pius XII originally intended to name Stepinac as the Cardinal-Priest for <a href="/wiki/San_Paolo_alla_Regola" title="San Paolo alla Regola">San Paolo alla Regola</a>, a titular cardinalatial title that the pontiff instituted in 1946, but had been vacant from its conception until the 1953 consistory. However, Stepinac was unable to travel to Rome to be invested with the title, therefore, it remained vacant until 1959 when it became a diaconate.<sup id="cite_ref-SP_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SP-246">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup> On 21 December 2020, the title's current holder, <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Monterisi" title="Francesco Monterisi">Francesco Monterisi</a>, inaugurated a plaque placed at the church to honor Stepinac.<sup id="cite_ref-SP_246-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SP-246">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_canonisation_controversies">Death and canonisation controversies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Aloysius_Stepinac&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Death and canonisation controversies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:177px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Blazeni_Kardinal_Alojzije_Stepinac_Rijeka_0308.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Blazeni_Kardinal_Alojzije_Stepinac_Rijeka_0308.jpg/175px-Blazeni_Kardinal_Alojzije_Stepinac_Rijeka_0308.jpg" decoding="async" width="175" height="510" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Blazeni_Kardinal_Alojzije_Stepinac_Rijeka_0308.jpg/263px-Blazeni_Kardinal_Alojzije_Stepinac_Rijeka_0308.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Blazeni_Kardinal_Alojzije_Stepinac_Rijeka_0308.jpg/350px-Blazeni_Kardinal_Alojzije_Stepinac_Rijeka_0308.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="1866" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Blazeni_Kardinal_Alojzije_Stepinac_Rijeka_0308.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Stained_glass" title="Stained glass">Stained glass</a> in the Church of Virgin Mary of Lourdes in <a href="/wiki/Rijeka" title="Rijeka">Rijeka</a></div></div></div>
<p>In 1953, two American specialists, Dr. <a href="/wiki/John_H._Lawrence" title="John H. Lawrence">John H. Lawrence</a> and Dr. John Ruzic, flew to Yugoslavia and diagnosed Stepinac with <a href="/wiki/Polycythemia" title="Polycythemia">polycythemia</a>, a disease marked by an increase in red blood cells. Stepinac was also treated for the disease by internationally known German hematologist, Dr. <a href="https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_Heilmeyer" class="extiw" title="de:Ludwig Heilmeyer">Ludwig Heilmeyer</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-247" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-247">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup>
On 10 February 1960 at the age of 61, Stepinac died of a <a href="/wiki/Thrombosis" title="Thrombosis">thrombosis</a>. <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_XXIII" title="Pope John XXIII">Pope John XXIII</a> held a requiem mass for him soon after at <a href="/wiki/St_Peter%27s_Basilica" class="mw-redirect" title="St Peter&#39;s Basilica">St Peter's Basilica</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-248" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-248">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup> He was buried in Zagreb during a service in which the protocols appropriate to his senior clerical status were, with Tito's permission, fully observed.<sup id="cite_ref-249" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-249">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup> Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Franz_K%C3%B6nig" title="Franz König">Franz König</a> was among those who attended the funeral.<sup id="cite_ref-250" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-250">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> Yugoslav government relations with the Vatican improved after Stepinac's death, and developed further after the <a href="/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council" title="Second Vatican Council">Second Vatican Council</a> of 1962–65. Diplomatic relations were restored in 1966.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWest1995286_239-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest1995286-239">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Notwithstanding that Stepinac died peacefully at home, he quickly became a <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a> in the view of his supporters and many other Catholics. In 1998, traces of arsenic were detected in Stepinac's bones, leading many to believe he had been poisoned by his captors.<sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> But administration of arsenic along with bloodletting was a standard treatment for polycythemia in the early 1950s.<sup id="cite_ref-253" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-253">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Meštrović did not return to Yugoslavia until 1959 and upon his return met again with Stepinac, who was then under house arrest.<sup id="cite_ref-254" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-254">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> Meštrović went on to sculpt a bust of Stepinac after his death which reads: "Archbishop Stepinac was not a man of idle words, but rather, he actively helped every person─when he was able, and to the extent he was able. He made no distinctions as to whether a man in need was a Croat or a Serb, whether he was a Catholic or an Orthodox, whether he was Christian or non-Christian. All the attacks upon him be they the product of misinformation, or the product of a clouded mind, cannot change this fact."<sup id="cite_ref-Gitman,_Elise_130-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gitman,_Elise-130">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1970, <i><a href="/wiki/Glas_Koncila" title="Glas Koncila">Glas Koncila</a></i> published a text on Stepinac taken from <i><a href="/wiki/L%27Osservatore_Romano" title="L&#39;Osservatore Romano">L'Osservatore Romano</a></i> which resulted in the edition being confiscated by court decree.<sup id="cite_ref-255" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-255">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 1979, Archbishop <a href="/wiki/Franjo_Kuhari%C4%87" title="Franjo Kuharić">Franjo Kuharić</a> told Croatian pilgrims and <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> at <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's Basilica</a> in Rome that Cardinal Stepinac should be <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatified</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> The beatification process began on 9 October 1981.<sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup>  The Catholic Church declared Stepinac a martyr on 11 November 1997,<sup id="cite_ref-Patron_Saints_Index_258-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Patron_Saints_Index-258">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> and on 3 October 1998 <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a>, on pilgrimage to Marija Bistrica to beatify Stepinac, declared that Stepinac had indeed been martyred.<sup id="cite_ref-259" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-259">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> John Paul had earlier determined that where a candidate for sainthood had been martyred, his/her cause could be advanced without the normal requirement for evidence of a miraculous intercession by the candidate. Accordingly, he <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatified</a> him.
</p><p>The beatification re-ignited old divisions between Serbs, who are mainly <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a>, and Croats. Shortly prior, the Paris-based <a href="/wiki/Simon_Wiesenthal_Center" title="Simon Wiesenthal Center">Simon Wiesenthal Center</a> requested that the Holy See delay the beatification until the case was given further study only to infuriate Croatian officials as well as the Vatican.<sup id="cite_ref-stanley_260-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-stanley-260">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Ljubojević, Gavrilović and Perica, the mythology regarding Stepinac was created during the <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> and newly independent <a href="/wiki/Croatia" title="Croatia">Croatia</a> with the cardinal's beatification in 1998. Their assessment is that this myth positioned Stepinac as the primary character in Croatian mythology, crediting him as a hero and martyr who was politically impartial. This myth alleges that Stepinac resisted all forms of totalitarianism in equal measure. The authors claim that Stepinac was a much greater opponent of communism than he was of Nazism and fascism, but his story was used by Croatian President <a href="/wiki/Franjo_Tudjman" class="mw-redirect" title="Franjo Tudjman">Franjo Tudjman</a> to legitimise Croatian independence and to bolster the role of the Catholic Church as a central pillar of Croatian statehood.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELjubojevićGavrilovićPerica201167–68_261-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELjubojevićGavrilovićPerica201167–68-261">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The statement by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Francis" title="Pope Francis">Pope Francis</a> regarding the canonisation of Cardinal Stepinac made in early May 2019, in which he said he had sought advice and help of the "great" <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">Serbian</a> Patriarch <a href="/wiki/Irinej,_Serbian_Patriarch" title="Irinej, Serbian Patriarch">Irenaeus</a> on the issue,<sup id="cite_ref-262" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-262">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-263" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-263">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> elicited a formal reaction from Archbishop <a href="/wiki/%C5%BDelimir_Pulji%C4%87" title="Želimir Puljić">Želimir Puljić</a>, President of the <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_Conference_of_Croatia" title="Episcopal Conference of Croatia">Croatian Conference of Bishops</a>, in which he stated that the involvement of the Serbian Orthodox Church in Stepinac's canonisation represented a precedent in the Catholic Church.<sup id="cite_ref-265" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-265">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-266" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-266">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup> In an interview that was published on the official information web site of the Catholic Church in Croatia on 24 May, the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Gospi%C4%87-Senj" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Gospić-Senj">Bishop Emeritus of Gospić-Senj</a>, Mile Bogović, called such move on the part of the Pope ″not good for the Church″.<sup id="cite_ref-267" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-267">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup>
